Patrologia Latina · Vol. 113 ·

LIBER GENESIS

One book of the Glossa ordinaria — the whole gloss, book by book.

PL 113, coll. 67–182  ·  Latin from the Migne printing  ·  New English translation  ·  column numbers follow the original plates, not the Garnier reprint

Anselm of Laon and his school

Anselmus Laudunensis et schola

twelfth century

The masters of the cathedral school of Laon, gathered around Anselm (c. 1050–1117), to whom the making of the Glossa ordinaria on the Bible is traditionally credited. The gloss is a school's work rather than one man's: it draws its comment from the Fathers — Rabanus, Isidore, Bede, Jerome, Gregory — and names them as it goes, and it grew by accretion over generations. Migne prints it under the name of Walafrid Strabo, an ascription now known to be mistaken.

Hebraice dictus בראשית , BERESIT, id est In principio; Graece ΓΕΝΕΣΙΣ, id est Generatio.

Called in Hebrew בראשית , BERESIT, that is In the beginning; in Greek ΓΕΝΕΣΙΣ, that is Generation.

CAPUT PRIMUM.

  • 67B

VERS. 1.-- « In principio creavit, » etc. Non dicit: In principio dicit Deus, Fiat coelum et terra. Sed dixit: « Fiat lux, et facta est lux, » quoniam universaliter nomine coeli et terrae comprehendendum erat quidquid fecit Deus, deinde per partes explicandum quomodo fecit. Unde sequitur: « Dixit Deus, Fiat, » id est, per verbum suum fecit. Vel quia cum primum fiebat informis materia spiritualis, vel corporalis, non erat dicendum: « Dixit Deus, Fiat; » incongruum enim erat a Deo dici, « fiat, » quia formam verbi non imitatur imperfectio, nisi cum generis sui conversione ad creatorem sit perfecta creatura; ut cum dicitur: « Dixit Deus, Fiat, » intelligamus eum ad se imperfectionem creaturae revocare. 67CCum ergo dicitur: « In principio fecit Deus coelum et terram, » commemoratio Filii fit, quia principium est; sed cum dicitur: « Dixit Deus, Fiat, » commemoratur quia Verbum est. Per principium notat exordium creaturae existentis ab illo; per Verbum, perfectionem creaturae ab illo ad ipsum revocatae, ut formetur imitando formam Verbi incommutabilem. Non enim habet vitam informem Verbum Dei, cui est idem esse quod vivere; imo idem vivere quod beate vivere. Creatura vero spiritualis 68Bhabet informem vitam, cui non est hoc esse quod vivere; nec vivere quod beate vivere: quia, aversa a sapientia Dei, stulte et misere vivit.

« In principio, » etc. (BEDA, Hexaem. lib. I, tom. II, col. 13 ª.) Creationem mundi insinuans Scriptura, primo verbo aeternitatem et omnipotentiam Dei ostendit: quem enim in principio temporum mundum creasse perhibet, eumdem ante tempora aeternaliter significat exstitisse. Et quem in conditionis initio coelum et terram creasse narrat, tanta celeritate operationis omnipotentem esse declarat, cui voluisse, facere est. Bene ergo dicitur: « In principio creavit, » etc. Utrumque scilicet, simul, quamvis utrumque simul ab homine non dicatur. Unde propheta terram in initio factam ostendit dicens, 68Cpsal. CI: « Initio tu, Domine, terram fundasti, » etc.

« In principio, » etc. Coelum non visibile firmamentum, sed empyreum, id est, igneum vel intellectuale, quod non ab ardore, sed a splendore dicitur, quod statim repletum est angelis. Unde in Job XXXVIII: « Ubi eras cum me laudarent astra matutina, » etc. Et nota tria hic commemorari elementa. Nomine coeli, aerem intelligimus. Nomine terrae, ipsam et ignem qui in ea latet. Quarti, id est, aquae in sequentibus fit mentio.

69A(ALCUIN. in Gen. tom. I.) « In principio. » Filio, quo humanato patuit qui essent coelestes, qui terreni. Coelum, creatura spiritualis, ab exordio perfecta et beata. Terra, corporalis materies adhuc imperfecta. Coelum, informem materiam spiritualis vitae, sicut in se potest existere non conversa ad Creatorem in quo formatur. Terram, corporalem, sine omni qualitate quae apparet in materia formata.

(BEDA in Gen., tom. II, col. 191.) Scriptura ait: « Qui fecisti mundum de materia informi (Sap. XI). » Materia facta est de nihilo. Species mundi de materia informi. Proinde duas res fecit Deus ante omne tempus, angelicam creaturam et materiam informem. Quamvis enim Salomon dicat: « Qui vivit in 69B« aeternum, creavit omnia simul (Eccli. XVIII), secundum originem tamen informis materies antecedit formatam speciem. Itaque mundum, et angelos, et animam de nihilo fecit Deus: hominem et creaturas caeteras de aliquo.

(Ibid., col. 192.) Septem sunt coeli, quorum haec sunt nomina, aer, aether, olympus, spatium vel igneum, firmamentum, coelum angelorum, et Trinitatis. Hieronymus autem dicit coelum Trinitatis primum, secundum angelorum, tertium firmamentum.

Mystice. « In principio creavit Deus coelum. » Eos scilicet qui coelestis imaginem portaverunt. « Et terram, » id est eos qui postea superbiendo, terram, id est terreni hominis imaginem, portantes, se recerunt deformes. « Terra autem erat inanis, » quia 69Cdeposuerat formam bonam. « Et vacua, » boni operis fructu. « Et tenebrae erant, » veri scilicet luminis privatio; « super faciem abyssi, » corda scilicet superborum.

VERS. 2.-- « Terra autem erat inanis, » etc. (BEDA, Hexaem. tom. II.) Quod vel quale coelum in principio cum terra factum est, ostendit. Hoc enim de terra subdit, quod de coelo intelligi noluit. Hoc enim superius coelum quod a volubilitate mundi secretum est, mox ut creatum est, sanctis angelis est impletum: quos in principio cum coelo et terra conditos testatur Dominus dicens Job XXXVIII: « Ubi eras cum me laudarent astra matutina, et jubilarent omnes filii Dei? » Astra matutina eosdem angelos et filios Dei vocat. De coelo enim in 69Dquo posita sunt luminaria, quomodo vel quando factum sit postea dicit.

(STRAB.) « Inanis et vacua. » Inutilis scilicet, et infructuosa, et incomposita. Omnia enim elementa commixta, confusa, et totum hoc aeris spatium aquis plenum; non quales nunc sunt, sed sicut nebulae tenues erant, quales adhuc supercoelestes sunt.

(BEDA ubi supra.) « Tenebrae erant, » etc. Non sunt audiendi qui reprehendendo dicunt Deum prius creasse tenebras quam lucem: quia nullas in aqua, vel aere fecit tenebras; sed distincto ordine providentiae prius aquas cum coelo creavit, et terra, et has cum voluit lucis gratia venustavit. Et notandum quod cum coelo duo elementa mundi creata sunt, 70Aquibus alia duo inserta sunt, aqua, scilicet, et terra, quibus insunt ignis et aer. Aquae autem totam superficiem terrae tanta altitudine tegebant, ut ad illos usque locos pertingerent, ubi nunc usque super firmamentum partim resident. Ipsa autem terra et aqua informis dicuntur materia, quia omnia quae videmus, vel ex istis sumpserunt exordium, vel ex nihilo: et priusquam in lucem venirent, non erat unde formam haberent.

« Et Spiritus Domini ferebatur super aquas. » (AUG., lib. I de Gen. ad lit., c. 5, tom. III.) Sive totam corporalem materiam aquam appellavit, ut insinuaret unde facta et formata sunt omnia quae ex humida natura per species varias in terra formari videmus; sive spiritualem vitam ante conversionis 70Bformam quasi fluitantem. Spiritus Dei superferebatur, quia subjacebat bonae voluntati Creatoris, quod formandum et perficiendum inchoaverat. Dictum est etiam « Spiritus Domini ferebatur super aquas. » Ne faciendo opera potius per necessitatem indigentiae quam per abundantiam beneficentiae Deus amare putaretur. Commode enim prius insinuatur aliquid inchoatum, cui superferretur: non loco, sed omnia superante potentia.

« Et Spiritus Domini ferebatur, » etc. (HIERON. in Gen. t. III, col. 939.) In Hebraeo habet merahephet, id est, incubabat, vel fovebat, more volucris ova calore animantis. Intelligimus ergo non de spiritu mundi dici, ut putant multi; sed de Spiritu 70Csancto, qui et ipse est omnium vivificator: si autem vivificator, et conditor; si conditor, et Deus. Psal. CIII: « Emitte, ait, Spiritum tuum et creabuntur. »

« Et Spiritus, » etc. In quo subsistentes requiesceremus flatu ejus vivificati, et unda baptismi abluti. « Et Spiritus Domini ferebatur super aquas, » etc. Sicut Deus, scilicet et conditor, praeerat fluitanti et confusae materiei, ut distingueret quando vellet. Tota ergo Trinitas hic operata intelligitur, Deus, Pater scilicet; principium, Filius; Spiritus Dei, Spiritus sanctus.

« Et Spiritus, » etc. (ISID., in Gen.? ) Corda scilicet fluctuantia, quae mentis quietem amiserant, 70Dquia eis spiritus non innitebatur, sed superferebatur: quasi potens naturam a confusione in melius revocare. Deinde per totum hujus Scripturae textum sex quasdam operosas aetates videmus, licet quasi proximis limitibus distinctas, ut in septima quies speretur; et eas habere similitudinem sex dierum, quibus ea facta sunt, quae Deum fecisse Scriptura commemorat. Primordium enim generis humani, quo ista luce frui coepit, comparatur primo diei quo lucem Deus fecit. Haec aetas tanquam infantia est totius saeculi, quod tanquam unum hominem proportione magnitudinis suae cogitamus. Homo enim cum primo nascitur in lucem, infantiam agit primam aetatem, quae tendit ab Adam usque ad Noe generationibus decem: diluvium quasi vespera hujus 71Adiei est, quia infantia nostra oblivionis diluvio deletur.

VERS. 3.-- « Dixitque Deus: Fiat lux. » (AUG., de Gen. ad litt., lib. I, c. 17, tom. III.) Lux primo die facta spiritualis vel corporalis intelligitur, etc., usque ad mane futuri inchoatio. (BEDA, Hexaem. ) Si autem primo die corporalis lux facta est, congrue mundi ornatus a luce inchoatur, unde caetera, quae creanda erant, videbantur. Si autem quaeritur, ubi est facta, cum abyssus omnem terrae altitudinem tegeret? patet quod in illis partibus, quas nunc illustrat solis diurna lux. Nec mirum lucem in aquis posse lucere, cum etiam nautarum operatione saepius illustrentur, qui in profundo mersi, misso ex ore oleo, aquas sibi illustrant: quae tunc multo rariores 71Bquam sint modo, fuere in principio, quia nondum congregatae in uno loco.

« Dixitque Deus: Fiat lux. » (AUG. ibid., c. 2.) Non temporaliter: si enim temporaliter, et mutabiliter; si mutabiliter, et per subjectam creaturam, non est lux prima creatura. Sed forsitan, etc., usque ad ut sit et ut maneat.

« Fiat lux. » (AUG. ibid., c. 4, 5, 9.) Ea conditione scilicet qua cuncta subsistunt intemporaliter in Dei sapientia, priusquam in seipsis. « Et facta est lux, » id est angelica et coelestis substantia, in se temporaliter; sicut erat in sapientia, quantum ad ejus incommutabilitatem, aeternaliter. Vel notatur hujus creaturae informitas scilicet et imperfectio antequam formaretur in amore Conditoris: formatur enim cum 71Cconvertitur ad incommutabile lumen Verbi.

« Fiat lux. » (AUG. ibid., c. 10, 11, 16.) Si corporaliter lux facta est, etc., usque ad nondum enim erant animalia quibus haec vicissitudo exhiberetur.

VERS. 4.-- « Et vidit Deus lucem, » etc. (STRAB.) Lucem et tenebras fecit Deus, unde Dan. III. « Benedicite, lux et tenebrae, Domino, » cum omnis creatura ad benedicendum invitatur.

(ISID., in Gen., c. 2.) Habet quisque in operibus et recta vita, distinctos sex dies, post quos requiem speret: primo die, lucem fidei, quoniam primo invisibilibus credit propter quam fidem Dominus apparere dignatus est.

« Factumque est vespere. » (AUG., de Gen. contra Manich., l. I, c. 4; BEDA, in Hexaem.) Occidente luce 71Dpaulatim, et post spatium divinae longitudinis inferiores partes subeunte, factum est vespere, sicut nunc usitato circumitu solis solet fieri. Factum est autem mane eadem super terram redeunte, et alium diem inchoante, et dies expletus est unus viginti quatuor horarum. Notandum vero quod nox in illo triduo omnino tenebrosa fuit, quae post creata sidera aliqua luce claruit: decebat etiam ut dies a luce inciperent, et in mane sequentis diei tenderent, ut opera Dei a luce inchoata et in lucem completa esse significarentur.

VERS. 5.--. . . . « Factumque est vespere, » etc. (GREG., l. VIII Moral., c. 6, tom. I.) Nequaquam in hac vita per exercitationem justitiae peccatum deseritur, 72Aut in ipsa inconcusse maneatur: quia si culpam rectitudo eliminat, cogitationis nostrae foribus assidens, ut sibi aperiatur, pulsat. Unde ait Moyses: « Facta est lux; » et paulo post: « Factum est vespere. » Creator enim, humanae culpae praescius, tunc expressit in tempore quod nunc versatur in mente. Lumen namque rectitudinis sequitur umbra tentationis. Sed quia electorum lux tentatione non exstinguitur, non nox, sed vespera facta memoratur, quia tentatio lumen justitiae abscondit, non interimit.

(AUG., de Gen. ad litt., l. IV, c. 22, 23, tom. III, col. 311, 312.) Notandum, quod caeterae creaturae, etc., usque ad ut illud scilicet ad diem pertineat, hoc ad vesperam.

72BVERS. 6.--. . . « Fiat firmamentum, » etc. (GREG., l. XXVII Moral., c. 24.) Angelicae virtutes, quae in Dei amore perstiterunt, hoc in retributione acceperunt, ut in contemplatione Conditoris perenni felicitate maneant, et in hoc quod conditae sunt aeternaliter subsistant. Unde apud Moysen coelum factum dicitur, et idem postea firmamentum vocatur, quia angelica natura prius subtilis est in superioribus condita, et post, ne unquam cadere possit, mirabilius confirmata.

« Fiat firmamentum, » etc. (AUG., l. II de Gen. ad litt., c. 2, col. 264.) Quaeritur utrum illud coelum nunc fiat quod excedit aeris spatia, etc., usque ad quia intervallum ejus dividit inter quosdam vapores aquarum et aquas quae sunt in terris.

72C« Fiat firmamentum, » etc. (AUG., ibid., c. 4.) Notandum quod firmamentum non propter stationem, etc., usque ad sed quoquomodo ibi sint, ibi esse non dubitamus.

« Fiat firmamentum in medio. » (BEDA, in Hexaem., t. II.) Coeli, in quo fixa sunt sidera, etc., usque ad Quales autem, et ad quid utiles, novit qui condidit.

VERS. 7.-- « Et fecit Deus firmamentum, » etc. (AUG., l. II de Gen. ad litt., c. 8, col. 269.) Quaeritur quare cum dixisset: « Fiat firmamentum et factum est ita, » postea addit: « Et fecit Deus firmamentum, » etc., usque ad dicens: « Dixitque Deus fiat, » etc.

(Ibid., c. 1, 3, 5.) Super aerem purus ignis, etc., usque ad Facit ergo frigidam aquarum supercoelestium 72Dvicinitas.

(Ibid.) Notandum vero quod prima die cum facta est lux, dictum est tantum: « Fiat lux, » et hoc in verbo, et « facta est lux, » in opere, statimque adhaesit increatae luci lux creata, ut vicinior et omni creatura capacior. In secunda die quaedam mora est, et verborum multiplicatio, « fiat, » et « fecit Deus, » et « factum est ita » : ut intelligamus istas naturas ad deficiendum pronas, et ad obediendum minus aptas.

(HIERON., l. I contra Jovin., t. II, col. 211.) Notandum etiam quod hujus diei secundi opera secundum Hebraeos non dicuntur bona, cum bona sint sicut caetera: quod fit propter binarium principem alternitatis, 73Aqui primus ab unitate discedit: et in figura bigamiae ponitur et multorum reprehensibilium; unde quae bina ingrediuntur in arcam immunda sunt, impar numerus mundus. Vel ideo bona non dicuntur, quia in hoc opere distinctio aquae non fit perfecta, quae in proximo fit, ubi dicit: « Congregentur aquae, » ut ibi dicetur.

(AUG., ubi supra, c. 9.) Quaeri solet cujus figurae sit coelum: sed Spiritus sanctus, quamvis auctores nostri scierint, per eos docere noluit, nisi quod prosit saluti. Quaeritur etiam si stet an moveatur coelum? Si moveatur, inquiunt, quomodo est firmamentum? Si stat, quomodo sidera in eo fixa circumeunt? Sed firmamentum non propter stationem, sed propter firmitatem, aut terminum aquarum intransgressibilem 73Bdici potest. Si autem stat, nihil impedit moveri et circumire sidera. Secundo die factum est tanquam firmamentum disciplinae, quod discernit inter carnalia et spiritualia, sicut inter inferiores et superiores aquas.

(ISID., in Gen. c. 2, tom. V, col. 213.) Die secunda posuit firmamentum, sanctas scilicet Scripturas in Ecclesia. De quibus dicitur Isa. XXXIV: « Coelum sicut liber plicabitur. » Hoc firmamento discernit aquas superiores, id est populum angelorum, quibus non est necesse ut in lectione audiant Dei verbum. Sed illud firmamentum posuit super infirmitatem inferiorum populorum, ut suspicientes in eo cognoscant qualiter discernat inter carnalia et spiritualia, quasi inter aquas superiores et inferiores.

73C« Divisitque aquas quae erant, » etc. (AMBR., l. II Hexaem., c. 3, tom. I, col. 148.) Cum haec audis, quid miraris si supra firmamentum potuit unda suspendi? etc., usque ad Jordanis quoque reflexo amne in suum fontem revertitur.

VERS. 8.--. . . . « Et factum est vespere, » etc. (ISID., ubi supra.) Incipit mane secundae diei a Noe, etc., usque ad quia nec pueritia apta est generationi.

VERS. 9.-- « Dixit vero Deus, » etc. (AUG., lib. II de Gen. ad litt., c. 11, tom. III.) In hoc die factum videtur, etc., usque ad Unde aqua congregatur, terra apparet; aqua enim labilis et fluxa, terra stabilis et fixa.

« Congregentur aquae? » (BEDA, in Hexaem. ) Quae inter coelum et terram universa compleverant, in 73Dunum locum congregantur, ut lux, quae praeterito biduo aquas clara luce lustraverat, in puro aere clarior fulgeat; et appareat terra, quae latebat; et quae aquis limosa erat, fiat arida et germinibus apta. Si quaeratur ubi congregatae sunt aquae, quae omnes partes terrae usque ad coelum texerant, potuit fieri ut terra subsidens concavas partes praeberet, quibus fluitantes aquas reciperet: potest etiam credi, primarias aquas rariores fuisse, quae sicut nebula tegerent terras, sed congregatione esse spissatas.

VERS. 10.-- « Et vocavit Deus aridam terram. » (BEDA, ibid.) Prius ad distinctionem aquarum totam solidiorem mundi partem terram appellavit dicens: « In principio creavit Deus coelum et terram. » Postquam 74Amundus jam formari incipit, et terrae facies apparet, recedentibus aquis, ad distinctionem partis quae adhuc premebatur aquis, portio quam aridam esse licebat, terrae nomen accepit, eo quod pedibus teratur. « Congregationesque aquarum appellavit maria. » Sic enim appellantur apud Hebraeos omnes congregationes aquarum, sive salsae, sive dulces. Et quia per continuationem omnium aquarum, quae in terris sunt, dixit eas congregatas in unum locum, nunc vocat pluraliter congregationes aquarum, propter multifidos sinus earum. Cum multa constat esse maria, in unum tamen locum dicit aquas congregatas, quia cuncta magno mari junguntur. Si qui lacus in semetipsis stricti videntur, occultis tamen meatibus in mare revolvuntur: fossores quoque puteorum 74Bhoc probant, quia omnis tellus per invisibiles venas aquis repleta est, quibus ex mari principium est.

VERS. 11.-- « Et ait: Germinet terra, » etc. (AUG., l. II de Gen. ad litt., c. 12.) Advertendum est moderamen ordinatoris: quamvis distincta est creatura herbarum atque lignorum a specie terrarum et aquarum, ut in elementis numerari non possint: quia tamen fixa radicibus terrae nectuntur, ad eumdem diem voluit pertinere.

(BEDA, ubi supra.) Patet in his verbis quod verno tempore mundus perfectus est et ornatus, in quo solent herbae virentes apparere, et ligna pomis onusta. Simulque notandum quod non prima herbarum germina vel arborum genera de semine prodierunt, sed de terra: quia ad unam jussionem Conditoris apparuit 74Carida herbis compta nemoribusque vestita, quae sui generis ex se poma produxerunt et semina. Oportebat enim ut forma rerum Dei imperio primo perfecta prodiret: quomodo homo, propter quem omnia, juvenili aetate plasmatus est.

(AUG., ibid. l. III, c. 18.) Quaeritur etiam de spinis, et tribulis, et quibusdam lignis infructuosis, cur vel quando creata sint? cum Deus dixerit: « Germinet terra herbam, » etc. Sed fructus nomine quaedam utilitas signatur: multae autem utilitates sunt manifestae, vel occultae, omnium quae terra radicitus alit. Possumus autem absolute respondere spinas et tribulos post peccatum terram homini ad laborem peperisse: non quod alibi antea nascerentur, et post in agris quos homo coleret: sed prius et postea in eisdem 74Dlocis, prius tamen non homini, sed post. Unde « pariet tibi, » id est, ut tibi nasci incipiant ad laborem, quae ad pastum tantummodo aliis animalibus ante nascebantur.

« Facientem semen. » (BEDA, ibid.) Omnia creata sunt perfecta, et homo perfectus aetate erat, et arbores cum foliis et fructu: unde dicitur hoc « lignum pomiferum. »

VERS. 12.-- « Et protulit terra herbam, » etc. (GREG., l. XIX Moral., c. 12; l. VI, c. 16, tom. I.) Quod testatur historia, sic est veraciter factum, ut significaret aliud faciendum. Terra enim est Ecclesia, quae verbi pabulo nos reficit, et patrocinii umbraculo custodit: loquendo pascit, opitulando protegit; 75Aut non solum herbam refectionis proferat, sed etiam arborem protectionis.-- « Lignumque. » Lignum secundum speciem suam semen produxit, cum mens nostra ex sui consideratione quod in alterum faciat colligit, et recti operis germen parit. Hinc quidam sapiens ait: « Quod tibi non vis fieri, alii non feceris (Tob. IV). » Et alibi: « Quaecunque vultis ut faciant vobis homines, et vos eadem illis facite (Matth. VII). » Quasi speciem vestram alterutrum respicite, et ex vobismetipsis cognoscite quid oporteat vos aliis exhibere.

Sementem. » (STRAB., RAB.) Sementis, frugum et arborum; semen, hominum, et animalium. Seminium vel sementum, cujusque rei exordium. Tertio die, quo mentem suam ad serendos bonorum operum 75Bfructus praeparat homo: separata labe, ac fluctibus carnalium tentationum, tanquam aridam habet terram tentationibus separatis, ut dicere possit: « Mente servio legi Dei, » etc. (Rom. VII).

VERS. 13.-- « Et factum est vespere et mane. » (ISID. in Gen., tom. V, col. 207.) Mane tertii diei incipit ab Abraham, etc., usque ad Vespera in peccatis populi quibus divina mandata praeteribant usque ad Saulis malitiam.

(ISID., ibid.) Die tertia congregavit aquas inferiores et salsas, infideles scilicet, concupiscentiarum et tentationum fluctibus sese quatientes: et segregavit ab eis aridam, id est, fideles fontem fidei sitientes. Infideles vero coercuit, ne iniquitatis suae fluctibus aridam, id est, animam sitientem Deum, conturbent, 75Csed liceat ei germinare fructus bonorum operum, et diligere proximum, ut habeat in se semen secundum similitudinem suam; ut scilicet ex sua infirmitate compatiatur alii indigenti, et producat lignum forte fructiferum, id est, beneficum, eripiendo oppressum, et praebendo ei protectionis umbraculum.

VERS. 14.-- « Dixit autem Deus: Fiant luminaria, » etc. (AUG., de Gen. ad litt., lib. II, c. 13, 14.) Quia visibili mundo constituendo duo dies sunt attributi, supremae scilicet et infimae parti mundi: visibilibus, et mobilibus mundi partibus, quae intra mundum creantur, tres reliqui deputati sunt, etc., usque ad in inchoationem, seu principatum noctis, quod fit cum plena est.

Et dividant diem. » (BEDA, in Hexaem.) Ea scilicet 75Ddivisione, ut sol diem, luna noctem illuminet. Hoc enim factis sideribus ad augmentum primariae lucis accessit, ut etiam nox prodiret luminosa, lunae vel stellarum splendore radiata, quae hactenus tantum tenebras noverat. Et si enim nox tenebrosa videtur obscurato aere, qui terris proximus est, superiora tamen aeris spatia semper siderum fulgore sunt lucida.

(AUG., lib. II de Gen. ad litt., c. 18.) Solet quaeri utrum haec luminaria corpora sola sint, aut habeant rectores spiritus, et ab eis vitaliter inspirentur, sicut per animas animalium carnes animantur. Sed de tam obscura re nihil temere credendum.

« Et sint in signa, » etc. (BEDA, ibid.) Quia priusquam 76Asidera fierent, ordo temporum nullis notabatur indiciis, vel meridiana hora, vel quaelibet alia. Sunt ergo luminaria « in signa, » etc.; non quod a conditione horum tempora coeperint, vel dies vel anni; sed quia per ortus eorum, vel transitus, temporum, dierum annorumque signatur ordo.

VERS. 15.-- « Ut luceant, » etc. (BEDA, ibid.) Semper luminaria in firmamento lucent, sed opportunis temporibus terram illuminant. Aliquoties enim nebulosus aer obsistit: ortus etiam sol majori lumine lunam stellasque ne terram illuminent, impedit. Unde sic vocatur, quia solus obtusa luna, stellisque caeteris, per diem fulgeat terris.

VERS. 16.-- « Fecit Deus duo luminaria. » (BEDA, ibid.) Luminaria magna possumus accipere, non tam 76Baliorum comparatione quam per se; ut coelum dicitur magnum, et magnum mare. Sol enim magnus dicitur, quia complet orbem terrae suo calore: sicut luna suo lumine, quae in quacunque parte coeli fuerint, totam terram illuminant, et a cunctis videntur. Magnitudinis eorum magnum est argumentum, quod omnibus hominibus idem orbis eorum videtur: nam si longe positis minor et prope major, exiguitatis indicium esset.

« Luminare majus, » etc. (BEDA, ibid.) Solem, scilicet non solum corporis forma, sed luminis magnitudine, quia luminare minus et stellas illustrat. Major est etiam in calore, quia mundum calefacit, cum praeteritis ante ejus creationem diebus nihil caloris habuisset. Quod autem aequalis uniusque magnitudinis 76Cluna cum sole cernitur, hinc esse aiunt quod multo longinquior a terris atque altior quam luna incedit, ideoque magnitudo ejus a nobis non posset dignosci. Omnia enim longius posita videntur breviora.

« Et luminare minus, » etc. Et si lunam et stellas majores in die aliquando videri contingat, non tamen diei, sed nocti lucis solatium eas afferre constat.

(AUG., de Gen. ad litt. lib. II, c. 15, tom. III.) Tunc enim in noctis exordio videtur luna, cum plena est, alias non: et per diem incipit videri ante plenitudinem, et in progressu noctis.

VERS. 17, 18-- . . . . « Ut lucerent, » etc. Haec de magnis luminaribus intelligi possunt et stellis; ut quod dictum est, « ut praeessent diei, » ad solem specialiter, 76Dquod subjunctum est, « et nocti, » ad lunam et stellas pertineat; quod vero additur, « dividerent lucem ac tenebras, » omnibus aeque conveniat; quae quocunque incedunt, lucem circumferunt; unde cum absunt, tenebrosa cuncta relinquunt. Si quaeratur quale potuit esse lumen diurnum ante creationem siderum, respondemus quale videmus solis ortu proximante, necdum apparente: quando scilicet lucet obtusis radiis stellarum dies, sed minime sol ortus refulget.

(ISID., in Gen., tom. V, col. 207.) Die quarta micuerunt luminaria in firmamento coeli, etc., usque ad quia post bona opera venit illuminatio, qua videtur species supernae veritatis.

77A(AUG., de Gen. contra Manich., lib. I, c. 24, tom. III, col. 194.) Quarto die, quo in illo firmamento disciplinae mens spirituales intelligentias operatur atque distinguit, videt quae sit incommutabilis veritas, quae sicut sol fulgeat in anima; et quomodo anima ipsius particeps fiat, et corpori ordinem et pulchritudinem tribuat, tanquam luna illuminans noctem. Et quomodo stellae omnes, id est intelligentiae spirituales, in hujus vitae obscuritate tanquam in nocte fulgeant.

VERS. 19.-- « Et factum est vespere et mane, » etc. (BEDA, in Hexaem., tom. II, col. 13.) In hac vespera populus Dei in Aegypto agnum obtulit, etc., usque ad spem atque fidem donaret.

« Et factum est vespere, » etc. (ISID., ubi supra.) 77BMane quartae diei regnum David. Haec aetas similis est juventuti, quae inter omnes regnat aetates, et firmum est omnium aetatum firmamentum: unde comparatur quarto diei, quo facta sunt sidera in firmamento coeli. Splendor siquidem solis regni figurat excellentiam, plebem obtemperantem regi lunae splendor ostendit, tanquam Synagogam. Stellae vero principes ejus sunt, et omnia fundata in regni stabilitate, tanquam in firmamento.

VERS. 20.-- « Dixit etiam Deus, » etc. (BEDA, ubi supra.) Post ornatam quarto die coeli faciem luminaribus, ornantur suis animalibus die quinto inferiores mundi partes, aqua scilicet et aer.

« Dixit etiam Deus: Producant aquae reptile, » etc. (AUG., lib. II de Gen. ad litt., ex c. 1, 2, 3, 4, 77C5 et 6, tom. III.) Oportebat in creandis habitatoribus inferioris partis, etc., usque ad Quod intelligendum non est nisi in aquarum naturam pinguioris aeris qualitate conversos.

« Animae viventis. » (AUG., ibid., c. 8.) Id est, quae sunt in animabus vivis, sicut dicitur: Ignobilia hominum, id est, qui sunt in hominibus ignobiles. Quod quidam propter tarditatem sensus putant dictum esse vel quia pisces nullius sint memoriae; sed certissimum est eos habere memoriam.

(AUG., ubi supra, c. 3, 4.) De conversione elementorum quaestio est. Alii dicunt omnia posse converti in omnia, etc., usque ad quod enim tactu aquas, ventosque sentimus; hinc est quod solida terra omnibus elementis miscetur, sed in his crassioribus 77Dsentitur amplius.

« Sub firmamento » , etc. Etsi immenso interjacente spatio, volant tamen aves sub sidereo coelo, quae super terram volant: sicut homines qui in terra sunt, sub coelo esse dicuntur. Alia translatio: « Secus firmamentum coeli, » quia vicina sunt aetheri spatia hujus aeris qui aves subvehit.

(AUG., de Gen. ad litt., l, III, c. 9, 10, tom. III, col. 284.) Notandum quosdam philosophos ita sua cuilibet elemento distribuisse animalia, etc., usque ad caliginosa tamen aeris loca tenere tantum permissi sunt, qui eis quasi carcer sit usque ad tempus judicii.

VERS. 21.-- « Creavitque Deus cete grandia, » etc. 78A(THEOD. q. 18 in Gen.) Pueri tum terriculamentis, tum loris etiam et virgis indigent: per illa ipsos terremus, per haec autem erudimus: Cum igitur animadvertit et nos Deus ad ignaviam proclives, bestias condidit, veluti lora quaedam et terriculamenta, ut per ea nos terrens ad se traheret, atque ejus auxilium imploraremus. At sicut qui perfecti sunt terriculamenta hujusmodi, et lora contemnunt; sic qui in virtute educati sunt, bestiarum incursus non formidant. Siquidem Adae, priusquam peccasset, assistebant bestiae, obsequium profitentes. Et rursum ipsum Noe ingredientem arcam, leo, pardus et bestiarum atrocissimae quaeque, ovium more, sequebantur. Et Danieli aderant leones, qui alioqui famelici, accedere tamen ad eum non audebant, eo 78Bquod in illo characteres divinam imaginem praeseferentes conspicerent. Itidem vipera, quae dentes injecerat in manum Apostoli, cum nihil peccato tenerum aut molle in eo reperisset, mox resiliit, et in ignem se conjecit, supplicium de seipsa quodammodo sumens, quod corpus sibi nullatenus conveniens adorta esset. Nos autem ideo bestias formidamus, quia virtutem negligimus. Praeterea et ad alia bestiae non sunt nobis inutiles: nam ex eis medici pharmaca non pauca componunt ad morbos pellendos: ne quis igitur bestias considerans de Creatore conqueratur, sed utilitatem investiget.

« Et omnem animam, » etc. Ad differentiam hominis, qui faciendus ad imaginem Dei, et, si praecepta servaret, perpetua immutabilitate maneret. 78CCaetera vero animalia sic facta sunt, ut alia aliis in alimoniam cederent vel senio deficerent.

VERS. 22.-- « Benedixitque illis, » etc. (AUG., lib. de Gen. imp., c. 15, tom. III.) Benedictionem ad fecunditatem valere voluit, quae in successione prolis apparet, ut ea benedictione qua infirma et mortalia creata sunt, genus suum nascendo custodiant.

(CHRYSOST., hom. 7 in Gen., tom. IV, col. 66.) Ipsa ergo benedictio est, in magno numero, et multitudine augeri, et multiplicari. Nam quia animatae creaturae erant animalia, volebantque perpetuo esse, ideo subdidit: « Crescite. » Verbum enim illud usque in praesentem diem illa conservat. « Crescite et multiplicamini, » etc. (BEDA, in Hexaem.) Ad utrumque 78Dgenus animantium de aquis factorum, id est pisces et aves, pertinet. Quia sicut omnes pisces nonnisi aquis vivere possunt,ita plurimae aves; quae si in terris aliquando requiescunt, fetusque faciunt, non tamen de terra, sed de mari vivunt, et libentius mari quam terra utuntur.

Mystice. (ISID. et AUG., ubi supra.) Die quinta facta sunt in aquis reptilia animarum vivarum, id est, homines in vitam renovati per sacramentum baptismi. Facta sunt volatilia, id est, animae volantes ad superna. Incipit provecta mens quinto die in actionibus turbulenti saeculi, tanquam in aquis maris operari, propter utilitatem fraternae societatis, et de corporalibus agnitionibus quae ad mare, id est 79Ahanc vitam, pertinent, producere animarum vivarum reptilia, id est opera quae prosunt animis vivis; et cetos magnos, id est fortissimas agnitiones, quibus fluctus saeculi dirumpuntur et contemnuntur; et volatilia coeli, id est, voces coelestia praedicantes.

VERS. 23.-- « Et factum est vespere et mane, etc. (AUG., de Gen. contra Manich., lib. II, c. 23, tom. III.) Mystice, mane transmigratio in Babyloniam, cum ea captivitate populus leviter in peregrino otio est collocatus, etc., usque ad quia sic excaecati sunt ut nec possent Christum cognoscere.

VERS. 24.-- « Dixit quoque Deus: Producat « terra, » etc. (BEDA, in Hexaem., tom. III, col. 13.) Post coelum repletum sideribus et aerem volatilibus qui propter viciniam coelum nominatur, etc., 79Busque ad nomine quadrupedum omnia comprehensa sunt praeter bestias et reptilia, sive domita, sive fera et agrestia.

« Producat terra, » etc. (AUG., lib. III in Gen., c. 11, tom. III.) Consequens erat ut terram jam ornaret suis animalibus. Ter dicendo, juxta genus suum, etc., usque ad Et nomine pecorum et bestiarum omne irrationale animal solet comprehendi. « In genere suo. » (AUG., ibid. c. 12,) Haec forsitan secundum genus dicuntur, quia ita orta ut ex eis nascantur alia, et originis formam successione conservent. De homine autem hoc dictum non est, quia unus fiebat, de quo et femina facta. Non enim multa genera hominum facta sunt, sicut herbarum et lignorum; ut dicatur, secundum genus suum, ac 79Csi diceretur generatim, ut inter similia, atque in unam originem seminis pertinentia distinguerentur a caeteris.

(AUG., ibid. c. 14.) De quibusdam minutis animalibus quaestio est, etc., usque ad nisi quod potentialiter in ipsis, et materialiter erant praeseminata.

(AUG., ibid. c. 15, 16.) De venenosis et perniciosis quaeritur, etc., usque ad quae cuncta merito considerata laudantur.

(AUG., ibid., c. 17.) Illud etiam movet, etc., usque ad sed piae fortitudinis numeros ad omnia praeparare non dubitent.

(THEOD., quaest. 18 in Gen.) Ne bestias, velut ab his, prorsus illaesi, contemnamus, quasi nullarum sint virium. Nonnunquam permittit Deus duos aut 79Dtres e multis millibus vel a scorpionibus pungi, vel a serpentibus morderi; ut nos veriti ne quid simile patiamur, Deum creatorem in auxilium invocemus.

(AUG. in Gen., l. III, c. 13.) Quaeritur quare hic non sit additum, quod dictum est de animalibus aquarum: « Benedixitque eis Deus dicens: Crescite et multiplicamini, » etc. Forte quod de prima creatura animae viventis dictum erat, de secunda subintelligendum reliquit, maxime quia in hujus diei operibus alia plura dicturus. Herbas autem atque ligna quia non habent generandi affectum, hujus benedictionis forsitan indigna judicavit. Quod necessario repetitum est in homine, ne quis putet 80Ain officio gignendi filios esse peccatum, sicut in fornicatione sine conjugio.

VERS. 25.-- « Et fecit Deus bestias terrae, » etc. (BEDA in Hexaem.) Ordinis est mutatio. Nec curandum in nostra loquela quid prius creaturarum ordine nominemus, cum Deus creaverit omnia simul.

(ISID., in Gen., tom. V, col. 207.) Sexta die producit terra animam vivam, quando caro nostra ab operibus mortuis abstinens, viva virtutum germina parit, secundum genus suum, id est, vitam imitando sanctorum. Unde I Cor. IV: « Imitatores mei estote, » etc. Secundum genus nostrum vivimus, quando sanctos quasi proximos imitamur. Producit terra bestias, id est, homines ferocitate superbientes. 80BEt pecora, id est, simpliciter viventes. Et serpentes, astutos scilicet et bonum a malo discernentes, et quasi reptando scrutantes terrena, per quae intelligant coelestia.

VERS. 26.-- « Faciamus hominem. » (AUG., ibid., c. 19.) Insinuatur pluralitas personarum Patris, et Filii, et Spiritus sancti, et statim unitas deitatis, cum dicitur: « Et fecit Deus hominem ad imaginem Dei, » non Pater ad imaginem tantum Filii. Non enim vere diceretur ad imaginem nostram; sed ita dictum est: « Fecit Deus hominem ad imaginem Dei, » ac si diceretur « ad imaginem suam. » Cum autem dicitur « ad imaginem Dei, » cum superius dictum sit, « ad imaginem nostram, » significatur quod non agit pluralitas personarum, ut 80Cplures deos credamus, sed ut Patrem, et Filium, et Spiritum sanctum unum Deum accipiamus.

« Faciamus hominem. » (BEDA, in Hexaem.) Apparet quare dictum sit ut caetera fierent, etc., usque ad sed ut veram post mortem se carnem recepisse monstraret.

« Et praesit, » etc. (AUG., lib. III de Gen. c. 20.) Notandum etiam quia cum dixisset « ad imaginem nostram, » continuo subjunxit: « Et praesit piscibus maris et volatilibus coeli. » Ut scilicet intelligamus in hoc factum hominem ad imaginem Dei, in quo irrationabilibus antecellit. Id autem est ratio, vel mens, vel intelligentia, vel si alio nomine commodius vocetur, in quo scilicet contemplandae veritati inhaeret.

80DVERS. 27.-- « Et creavit Deus hominem ad imaginem, » etc. (BEDA, in Gen., c. 1.) Qui non quemlibet sanctorum imitando, sed ipsam veritatem intuendo, operatur justitiam, ut ipsam veritatem intelligat et sequatur: hic accepit potestatem super omnia, quia spiritualis quisque effectus, et Deo similis, omnia judicat, et ipse a nemine judicatur.

« Et creavit Deus, » etc. (AUG., ubi supra, cap. 20, 21, 22.) Notandum quod sicut in creanda luce dicitur: « Fiat lux, » et statim sequitur, « et facta est lux; » sic cum dicitur: « Faciamus hominem, » infertur, « et fecit Deus hominem ad imaginem Dei: » quia ista natura intellectualis est, sicut illa lux: et hoc est ei fieri, quod 81Aagnoscere Verbum, per quod fit. etc., usque ad sed materialiter praeseminata.

« Masculum et feminam creavit eos, » etc. (BEDA, in Hexaem. ) Postea plenius et unde et quomodo homines facti sunt exponitur, etc., usque ad etsi virginitas praeferatur.

(BEDA, ISID., ut supra. ) Post haec « fecit Deus hominem ad imaginem et similitudinem suam, » virum scilicet perfectum, etc., usque ad unde Paulus: « Seminavimus spiritualia vobis, magnum est si carnalia vestra metamus (I Cor. IX). »

VERS. 28.-- . . . . . « Et dominamini piscibus, » etc. Quod prius quasi consulendo dixerat de eis, nunc dicit imperans, ut habet scilicet dominium omnium creaturarum in inferiore parte mundi. Quod per singula 81Bexsequitur.

« Et dominamini piscibus maris. » (BEDA, in Hexaem. ) Quaeritur qua utilitate homo dominatum inter caetera animantia percepit, etc., usque ad et rictus bestiarum et serpentum venenum cessisse.

VERS. 29.-- « Dixitque Deus: Ecce dedi, » etc. (BED., ibid.) Patet quod ante peccatum hominis terra nihil noxium protulit, non herbam venenatam, non arborem sterilem. Omnis enim herba et ligna data sunt hominibus, et volatilibus, et animantibus terrae in escam. Unde patet quod tunc animalia animalium esu non vivebant, sed concorditer herbis et fructibus vescebantur.

VERS. 30.-- . . . . . « Et factum est ita. » (AUG., de Gen. ad lit. lib. III, c. 13.) Notandum quod ubi datur 81Cpotestas homini herbas et fructus edendi, subinfertur, « et factum est ita: » in quo significatur quod hoc dicente Deo, haec sibi in esum concessa cognovit homo, non quod statim ederit. Si enim ad omnia supradicta referimus quod ait « et factum est ita, » consequens erit ut dicamus eos jam multiplicatos sexta die, quod post multos factum est annos.

VERS. 31.-- « Viditque Deus, » etc. (AUG., ibid.) Quaeritur, cum singillatim caetera bona dicantur, cur homo ad imaginem Dei factus cum caeteris hoc dicatur? Sed forte praesciebat Deus eum peccaturum, nec in perfectione imaginis mansurum. Qui enim singillatim bonus est, magis cum omnibus, sed non convertitur. Cautum est ergo ut diceretur, quod in praesenti verum esset, et praescientiam futuri significaret. 81DDeus autem omnia ordinavit, ut si qua singillatim fuerint delinquendo deformia, semper tamen cum eis universitas sit formosa.

« Et factum est vespere et mane, » etc. (ISID., in Gen., tom. V, col. 207.) Mane sexti diei fit praedicatio Evangelii per Christum, etc., usque ad qui vesperam non habet.

Moraliter. (AUG., de Gen. contra Manich., lib. I in fine.) Sexto die producit terra animam vivam, id est, homo de stabilitate suae mentis, ubi habet fructus spirituales, id est, bonas cogitationes; omnes motus animi sui regit, ut sit in illo anima viva, id est, rationi et justitiae serviens, non terrenitati et peccato. Ita fit homo ad imaginem et similitudinem 82ADei, masculus et femina; id est, intellectus et actio: quorum copulatione spiritualis fetus terram impleat, id est, carnem subjiciat, et caetera quae jam in hominis perfectione dicta sunt. In istis namque diebus vespera est ipsa perfectio singulorum operum, et mane inchoatio sequentium.

CHAPTER ONE.

  • 67B

VERS. 1.-- « In the beginning he created, » etc. He does not say: In the beginning God says, Let heaven and earth be made. But he said: « Let there be light, and light was made, » because under the name of heaven and earth there was to be comprehended universally whatsoever God made, and thereafter it was to be set forth part by part how he made it. Whence there follows: « God said, Let it be made, » that is, by his Word he made it. Or because, when formless matter, spiritual or corporeal, was first coming to be, it was not to be said: « God said, Let it be made; » for it was unfitting that « let it be made » should be said by God, since imperfection does not imitate the form of the Word, save when, by the conversion of its own kind to the Creator, the creature is made perfect; so that when it is said: « God said, Let it be made, » we may understand that he calls back to himself the imperfection of the creature. 67CWhen therefore it is said: « In the beginning God made heaven and earth, » [Vulg. Gen. 1:1 creavit, "created"] there is a commemoration of the Son, because he is the beginning; but when it is said: « God said, Let it be made, » it is commemorated that he is the Word. By "beginning" he notes the origin of the creature that exists from him; by "the Word," the perfecting of the creature called back from him to himself, that it may be formed by imitating the unchangeable form of the Word. For the Word of God has not a formless life, to whom to be is the same as to live; nay rather, to live the same as to live blessedly. But the spiritual creature 68Bhas a formless life, to whom to be is not the same as to live; nor to live the same as to live blessedly: because, turned away from the wisdom of God, it lives foolishly and wretchedly.

« In the beginning, » etc. (BEDA, Hexaem. lib. I, tom. II, col. 13 ª.) Scripture, in intimating the creation of the world, shows by its first word the eternity and the omnipotence of God: for him whom it declares to have created the world in the beginning of times, it signifies to have existed eternally before times. And him whom it relates to have created heaven and earth at the outset of the founding, it declares to be omnipotent by so great a swiftness of working, for whom to have willed is to do. Well therefore is it said: « In the beginning he created, » etc. Both, that is, at once, although both are not uttered at once by a man. Whence the prophet shows the earth to have been made in the beginning, saying, 68Cpsal. CI: « In the beginning thou, O Lord, didst found the earth, » etc.

« In the beginning, » etc. Heaven, not the visible firmament, but the empyrean, that is, the fiery or intellectual heaven, which is so called not from burning but from splendour, and which was straightway filled with angels. Whence in Job XXXVIII: « Where wast thou when the morning stars praised me, » etc. And note that three elements are here commemorated. By the name of heaven we understand the air. By the name of earth, the earth itself and the fire that lies hid in it. Of the fourth, that is, of water, mention is made in what follows.

69A(ALCUIN. in Gen. tom. I.) « In the beginning. » In the Son, by whose being made man it lay open who were the heavenly and who the earthly. Heaven, the spiritual creature, from its beginning perfect and blessed. Earth, corporeal matter, as yet imperfect. Heaven, the formless matter of spiritual life, such as it can exist in itself, not turned to the Creator in whom it is formed. Earth, the corporeal, without any quality that appears in formed matter.

(BEDA in Gen., tom. II, col. 191.) Scripture says: « Who madest the world of formless matter (Sap. XI). » [Vulg. Sap. 11:18 quae creavit orbem terrarum ex materia invisa, "which created the world out of matter unseen"] Matter was made out of nothing. The species of the world, out of formless matter. Accordingly God made two things before all time, the angelic creature and formless matter. For although Solomon says: « Who lives for 69B« ever created all things together (Eccli. XVIII), yet according to origin formless matter goes before formed species. And so God made the world, and the angels, and the soul out of nothing: man and the remaining creatures out of something.

(Ibid., col. 192.) There are seven heavens, whose names are these, air, ether, olympus, the space or fiery region, the firmament, the heaven of the angels, and that of the Trinity. Jerome, however, says that the heaven of the Trinity is the first, that of the angels the second, the firmament the third.

Mystically. « In the beginning God created heaven. » Those, namely, who have borne the image of the heavenly one. « And the earth, » that is, those who afterwards, by growing proud, bearing the earth, that is, the image of the earthly man, made themselves deformed. « But the earth was void, » because 69Cit had laid aside its good form. « And empty, » of the fruit of good work. « And there were darknesses, » the privation, namely, of the true light; « upon the face of the deep, » the hearts, namely, of the proud.

VERS. 2.-- « But the earth was void, » etc. (BEDA, Hexaem. tom. II.) He shows what heaven, or what kind of heaven, was made in the beginning together with the earth. For he subjoins that of the earth which he did not wish to be understood of heaven. For this higher heaven, which is set apart from the world's revolving, as soon as it was created was filled with holy angels: whom the Lord testifies to have been founded in the beginning together with heaven and earth, saying, Job XXXVIII: « Where wast thou when the morning stars praised me, and all the sons of God rejoiced? » He calls the same angels both morning stars and sons of God. For of the heaven in 69Dwhich the luminaries are set, how or when it was made, he tells afterwards.

(STRAB.) « Void and empty. » Useless, namely, and unfruitful, and unordered. For all the elements were mingled and confused, and this whole space of air was full of waters; not such as they now are, but they were like thin clouds, such as those above the heavens still are.

(BEDA ubi supra.) « There were darknesses, » etc. Those are not to be heard who say by way of reproach that God created darkness before light: because he made no darkness in the water or in the air; but by a distinct order of providence he first created the waters together with the heaven, and the earth, and adorned these with the grace of light when he willed. And it is to be noted that with the heaven two elements of the world were created, 70Ainto which two others were inserted, water, namely, and earth, in which are fire and air. And the waters covered the whole surface of the earth with so great a depth that they reached even to those places where until now they partly rest above the firmament. But the earth itself and the water are called formless matter, because all the things which we see took their beginning either from these or out of nothing: and before they came into the light, there was not whence they might have form.

« And the Spirit of the Lord was borne over the waters. » [Vulg. Gen. 1:2 Spiritus Dei, "the Spirit of God"] (AUG., lib. I de Gen. ad lit., c. 5, tom. III.) Either he called the whole corporeal matter water, that he might intimate whence were made and formed all the things which we see formed on the earth out of a moist nature through divers species; or the spiritual life, as it were floating, before the 70Bform of its conversion. The Spirit of God was borne above, because that which he had begun to be formed and perfected lay beneath the good will of the Creator. It is also said, « The Spirit of the Lord was borne over the waters. » Lest, by doing works, God should be thought to love through the necessity of want rather than through the abundance of beneficence. For fittingly is something first intimated to have been begun, over which he might be borne: not in place, but by a power surpassing all things.

« And the Spirit of the Lord was borne, » etc. (HIERON. in Gen. t. III, col. 939.) In the Hebrew it has merahephet, that is, it brooded, or cherished, after the manner of a bird warming eggs with the heat of a living thing. We understand therefore that this is not said of the spirit of the world, as many suppose; but of the 70CHoly Spirit, who is himself the quickener of all things: and if the quickener, also the founder; if the founder, also God. Psal. CIII: « Send forth, he says, thy Spirit, and they shall be created. » [Vulg. Ps. 103:30 Emittes, "Thou shalt send forth"]

« And the Spirit, » etc. In whom, subsisting, we might rest, quickened by his breath and washed by the wave of baptism. « And the Spirit of the Lord was borne over the waters, » etc. As God, namely, and Creator, he presided over the floating and confused matter, that he might distinguish it when he willed. The whole Trinity therefore is understood to have worked here, God, that is, the Father; the beginning, the Son; the Spirit of God, the Holy Spirit.

« And the Spirit, » etc. (ISID., in Gen.? ) The hearts, namely, that were tossing to and fro, which had lost the quiet of the mind, 70Dbecause the Spirit did not rest upon them, but was borne above them: as one able to call back their nature from confusion into something better. Then throughout the whole text of this Scripture we see certain six laborious ages, though distinguished as it were by neighbouring bounds, so that in the seventh rest may be hoped for; and we see that they have a likeness to the six days in which those things were made which Scripture records God to have made. For the first beginning of the human race, in which it began to enjoy this light, is compared to the first day, on which God made light. This age is as it were the infancy of the whole world, which we think of, by the proportion of its greatness, as one man. For man, when he is first born into the light, passes his infancy, the first age, which extends from Adam to Noah in ten generations: the flood is as it were the evening of this 71Aday, because our infancy is blotted out by the flood of forgetfulness.

VERS. 3.-- « And God said: Let there be light. » (AUG., de Gen. ad litt., lib. I, c. 17, tom. III.) The light made on the first day is understood as spiritual or corporeal, etc., as far as the beginning of a morning to come. (BEDA, Hexaem. ) But if corporeal light was made on the first day, fittingly is the adorning of the world begun from light, by which the rest, which were to be created, might be seen. But if it be asked: where was it made, since the deep covered the whole height of the earth? it is plain that it was made in those parts which the daily light of the sun now illumines. Nor is it a wonder that light can shine in waters, since they are even oftentimes lit up by the working of sailors, who, plunged in the deep, having let oil out of the mouth, light up the waters for themselves: which waters in the beginning were far rarer 71Bthan they now are, because they were not yet gathered into one place.

« And God said: Let there be light. » (AUG. ibid., c. 2.) Not temporally: for if temporally, then changeably also; if changeably, then also through a subject creature, and light is not the first creature. But perhaps, etc., as far as that it may be and that it may abide.

« Let there be light. » (AUG. ibid., c. 4, 5, 9.) On that condition, namely, by which all things subsist timelessly in the wisdom of God before they subsist in themselves. « And light was made, » that is, the angelic and heavenly substance, in itself temporally; as it was in wisdom, so far as regards his unchangeableness, eternally. Or there is noted the formlessness, namely, and the imperfection of this creature before it was formed in the love of its Founder: for it is formed when 71Cit is turned to the unchangeable light of the Word.

« Let there be light. » (AUG. ibid., c. 10, 11, 16.) If light was made corporeally, etc., as far as for there were not yet animals to which this vicissitude might be exhibited.

VERS. 4.-- « And God saw the light, » etc. (STRAB.) God made light and darkness, whence Dan. III. « Bless the Lord, O light and darkness, » when every creature is invited to bless.

(ISID., in Gen., c. 2.) Each one has in his works and in an upright life six distinct days, after which he may hope for rest: on the first day, the light of faith, since he first believes in invisible things, for which faith the Lord deigned to appear.

« And the evening was made. » (AUG., de Gen. contra Manich., l. I, c. 4; BEDA, in Hexaem.) The light going down 71Dlittle by little, and after the space of the divine length passing under to the lower parts, the evening was made, as it is now wont to come about by the accustomed circuit of the sun. And the morning was made when the same light returned over the earth and began another day, and one day of twenty-four hours was completed. But it is to be noted that the night in that three days' space was wholly dark, which after the stars were created shone with some light: it was fitting also that the days should begin from light and stretch toward the morning of the following day, so that the works of God might be signified to have been begun from light and completed unto light.

VERS. 5.--. . . . « And the evening was made, » etc. (GREG., l. VIII Moral., c. 6, tom. I.) In no wise in this life is sin so forsaken by the exercise of justice 72Athat one may abide in it unshaken: because, if uprightness drives out guilt, guilt sits at the doors of our thought and knocks, that it may be opened to it. Whence Moses says: « Light was made; » and a little after: « The evening was made. » For the Creator, foreknowing man's guilt, expressed then in time what is now busied in the mind. For the shadow of temptation follows the light of uprightness. But because the light of the elect is not put out by temptation, not night but evening is recorded to have been made, because temptation hides the light of justice, it does not destroy it.

(AUG., de Gen. ad litt., l. IV, c. 22, 23, tom. III, col. 311, 312.) It is to be noted that the other creatures, etc., as far as that the one, namely, may belong to the day, this to the evening.

72BVERS. 6.--. . . « Let there be a firmament, » etc. (GREG., l. XXVII Moral., c. 24.) The angelic powers which stood fast in the love of God received this in retribution, that they should abide in the contemplation of their Founder in everlasting happiness, and should subsist eternally in that wherein they were founded. Whence in Moses the heaven is said to have been made, and the same is afterwards called the firmament, because the angelic nature was first founded subtle in the higher places, and afterwards, that it might never be able to fall, was more wonderfully confirmed.

« Let there be a firmament, » etc. (AUG., l. II de Gen. ad litt., c. 2, col. 264.) It is asked whether that heaven which exceeds the spaces of the air is now made, etc., as far as because its interval divides between certain vapours of the waters and the waters which are on the earth.

72C« Let there be a firmament, » etc. (AUG., ibid., c. 4.) It is to be noted that the firmament is not so called on account of standing still, etc., as far as but in whatever manner they are there, that they are there we do not doubt.

« Let there be a firmament in the midst. » (BEDA, in Hexaem., t. II.) Of the heaven, in which the stars are fixed, etc., as far as But of what sort they are, and for what they are useful, he knows who founded them.

VERS. 7.-- « And God made the firmament, » etc. (AUG., l. II de Gen. ad litt., c. 8, col. 269.) It is asked why, when he had said: « Let there be a firmament, and it was so done, » he afterwards adds: « And God made the firmament, » etc., as far as saying: « And God said, Let it be made, » etc.

(Ibid., c. 1, 3, 5.) Above the air is pure fire, etc., as far as The nearness of the supercelestial waters therefore makes it 72Dcold.

(Ibid.) But it is to be noted that on the first day, when light was made, it was said only: « Let there be light, » and this in the word, and « light was made, » in the work, and straightway the created light clave to the uncreated light, as nearer to it and more capacious than every creature. On the second day there is a certain delay, and a multiplying of words, « let it be made, » and « God made, » and « it was so done » : that we may understand these natures to be prone to fail, and less apt to obey.

(HIERON., l. I contra Jovin., t. II, col. 211.) It is also to be noted that the works of this second day are not said, according to the Hebrews, to be good, although they are good like the rest: which comes about on account of the binary number, the chief of alternation, 73Awhich first departs from unity: and it is set as a figure of bigamy and of many reprehensible things; whence the things which enter into the ark two by two are unclean, an odd number clean. Or they are not said to be good for this reason, that in this work the separation of the water is not made perfect, which is made in the next, where he says: « Let the waters be gathered together, » as will be said there.

(AUG., ubi supra, c. 9.) It is wont to be asked of what shape the heaven is: but the Holy Spirit, although our authors knew, did not will to teach through them save what profits salvation. It is also asked: does the heaven stand still, or is it moved? If it is moved, they say, how is it a firmament? If it stands still, how do the stars fixed in it go round? But the firmament can be so called not on account of standing still, but on account of firmness, or the untransgressible boundary 73Bof the waters. But if it stands still, nothing hinders the stars from being moved and going round. On the second day there was made as it were the firmament of discipline, which distinguishes between carnal and spiritual things, as between the lower and the higher waters.

(ISID., in Gen. c. 2, tom. V, col. 213.) On the second day he set the firmament, that is, the holy Scriptures in the Church. Of which it is said, Isa. XXXIV: « The heaven shall be folded together like a book. » By this firmament he divides the higher waters, that is, the people of the angels, for whom there is no need that they should hear the word of God in reading. But he set that firmament above the infirmity of the lower peoples, that looking up at it they may know how it distinguishes between carnal and spiritual things, as it were between the higher and the lower waters.

73C« And he divided the waters which were, » etc. (AMBR., l. II Hexaem., c. 3, tom. I, col. 148.) When thou hearest these things, why dost thou marvel if above the firmament a wave could be hung? etc., as far as Jordan also, its stream turned back, returns into its own spring.

VERS. 8.--. . . . « And the evening was made, » etc. (ISID., ubi supra.) The morning of the second day begins from Noah, etc., as far as because neither is boyhood apt for generation.

VERS. 9.-- « And God said, » etc. (AUG., lib. II de Gen. ad litt., c. 11, tom. III.) On this day it seems to have been done, etc., as far as Whence the water is gathered together, the earth appears; for water is slippery and flowing, earth stable and fixed.

« Let the waters be gathered together? » (BEDA, in Hexaem. ) The waters which had filled all things between heaven and earth are gathered 73Dinto one place, that the light, which in the two days past had lit up the waters with a clear light, might shine brighter in the pure air; and that the earth might appear, which lay hidden; and that what was slimy with waters might become dry and apt for shoots. If it be asked where the waters were gathered together which had covered all parts of the earth up to the heaven, it could come about that the earth, subsiding, afforded hollow parts by which it might receive the floating waters: it can also be believed that the first waters were rarer, which covered the lands like a cloud, but that by the gathering they were thickened.

VERS. 10.-- « And God called the dry land Earth. » (BEDA, ibid.) Before, for the distinguishing of the waters, he called the whole more solid part of the world earth, saying: « In the beginning God created heaven and earth. » After 74Athe world now begins to be formed, and the face of the earth appears, the waters withdrawing, for the distinguishing of the part which was still pressed down by the waters, the portion which it was permitted to be dry received the name of earth, in that it is trodden by the feet. « And the gatherings together of the waters he called Seas. » For so among the Hebrews all gatherings of waters are called, whether salt or sweet. And because, by the continuity of all the waters which are on the earth, he said that they were gathered into one place, he now calls them in the plural the gatherings of waters, on account of their manifold bays. Although it is agreed that there are many seas, yet he says the waters were gathered into one place, because all are joined to the great sea. If any lakes seem to be shut up in themselves, yet by hidden passages they are rolled back into the sea: the diggers of wells 74Balso prove this, because all the ground is filled with waters through invisible veins, whose beginning is from the sea.

VERS. 11.-- « And he said: Let the earth bring forth, » etc. (AUG., l. II de Gen. ad litt., c. 12.) The governance of the Ordainer is to be marked: although the creature of herbs and of trees is distinct from the kind of the lands and of the waters, so that they cannot be numbered among the elements: yet because, fixed by their roots, they are bound to the earth, he willed them to belong to the same day.

(BEDA, ubi supra.) It is plain from these words that the world was perfected and adorned in the springtime, in which green herbs are wont to appear, and trees laden with fruits. And at the same time it is to be noted that the first shoots of herbs or kinds of trees came forth not from seed, but from the earth: because at one command of the Founder there appeared 74Cthe dry land trimmed with herbs and clothed with groves, which of themselves brought forth the fruits and seeds of their own kind. For it behoved that the form of things should come forth perfect at God's first command: as man, for whose sake are all things, was fashioned in a youthful age.

(AUG., ibid. l. III, c. 18.) It is also asked concerning thorns, and thistles, and certain unfruitful trees, why or when they were created? since God said: « Let the earth bring forth the green herb, » etc. But by the name of fruit a certain usefulness is signified: and there are many usefulnesses, manifest or hidden, of all the things which the earth nourishes by their roots. We can, however, answer without qualification that after sin the earth brought forth thorns and thistles for man's labour: not that they were born elsewhere before, and afterwards in the fields which man tilled: but before and afterwards in the same 74Dplaces, yet before not for man, but afterwards. Whence « it shall bring forth to thee, » that is, that there should begin to be born for thee unto labour the things which before were born only for the food of other animals.

« Making seed. » (BEDA, ibid.) All things were created perfect, and man was perfect in age, and the trees with leaves and fruit: whence this is called a « tree bearing fruit. »

VERS. 12.-- « And the earth brought forth the herb, » etc. (GREG., l. XIX Moral., c. 12; l. VI, c. 16, tom. I.) What history testifies was truly so done, that it might signify something else to be done. For the earth is the Church, which refreshes us with the food of the word, and guards us with the shade of its patronage: by speaking it feeds, by helping it protects; 75Aso that it may bring forth not only the herb of refreshment, but also the tree of protection.-- « And the tree. » The tree brought forth seed according to its own kind, when our mind gathers from the consideration of itself what it should do toward another, and bears the shoot of upright work. Hence a certain wise man says: « What thou wouldest not have done to thee, do not thou to another (Tob. IV). » And elsewhere: « Whatsoever you would that men should do to you, do you also the same to them (Matth. VII). » As if to say, look upon one another as your own species, and know from your own selves what it behoves you to show to others.

Seed. » (STRAB., RAB.) "Sementis" is of crops and of trees; "semen," of men and of animals. "Seminium" or "sementum," the beginning of anything whatever. On the third day, on which a man prepares his mind for the sowing of the 75Bfruits of good works: the taint being separated, and the waves of carnal temptations, he has as it were dry land, temptations being set apart, so that he can say: « I serve the law of God with the mind, » etc. (Rom. VII).

VERS. 13.-- « And the evening and the morning were made. » (ISID. in Gen., tom. V, col. 207.) The morning of the third day begins from Abraham, etc., as far as The evening in the sins of the people, by which they were transgressing the divine commandments, until the wickedness of Saul.

(ISID., ibid.) On the third day he gathered together the lower and salt waters, that is, the unbelievers, shaking themselves with the waves of concupiscences and of temptations: and he set apart from them the dry land, that is, the faithful thirsting for the fountain of faith. But the unbelievers he restrained, lest with the waves of their iniquity they should trouble the dry land, that is, the soul thirsting for God, 75Cbut that it might be free for it to bring forth the fruits of good works, and to love its neighbour, so that it may have in itself seed according to its own likeness; that is, that out of its own weakness it may have compassion on another in need, and may produce a strong fruit-bearing tree, that is, a beneficent one, by rescuing the oppressed and affording him the shade of its protection.

VERS. 14.-- « But God said: Let there be luminaries, » etc. (AUG., de Gen. ad litt., lib. II, c. 13, 14.) Because to the constituting of the visible world two days are assigned, namely to the highest and the lowest part of the world: to the visible and movable parts of the world, which are created within the world, the three remaining ones were allotted, etc., as far as unto the inchoation, or principate, of the night, which comes about when she is full.

And let them divide the day. » (BEDA, in Hexaem.) By that 75Ddivision, namely, that the sun should light the day, the moon the night. For this was added, when the stars were made, to the increase of the first light, that the night also might come forth luminous, irradiated by the splendour of the moon or of the stars, which hitherto had known darkness only. And although the night seems dark because the air which is nearest to the lands is darkened, yet the higher spaces of the air are always bright with the brightness of the stars.

(AUG., lib. II de Gen. ad litt., c. 18.) It is wont to be asked whether these luminaries are bodies only, or have ruling spirits, and are breathed upon by them vitally, as flesh is animated through the souls of animals. But concerning so obscure a matter nothing is rashly to be believed.

« And let them be for signs, » etc. (BEDA, ibid.) Because before 76Athe stars were made, the order of the times was marked by no tokens, neither the midday hour nor any other. The luminaries therefore are « for signs, » etc.; not that from the founding of these the times, or the days, or the years began; but because by their risings, or their passings, the order of times, of days and of years is marked.

VERS. 15.-- « That they may shine, » etc. (BEDA, ibid.) The luminaries always shine in the firmament, but at fitting times they illumine the earth. For sometimes the cloudy air stands in the way: the sun also, having risen, hinders the moon and the stars with its greater light from illumining the earth. Whence it is so called, because, the moon and the other stars being dulled, it alone shines upon the lands by day.

VERS. 16.-- « God made two luminaries. » (BEDA, ibid.) We can take them to be great luminaries, not so much 76Bby comparison with others as in themselves; as the heaven is called great, and the sea great. For the sun is called great, because it fills the globe of the earth with its heat: as does the moon with its light, which, in whatever part of the heaven they may be, illumine the whole earth, and are seen by all. A great argument of their greatness is that to all men the same orb of them appears: for if it were smaller to those placed afar and greater to those near, it would be an indication of littleness.

« The greater luminary, » etc. (BEDA, ibid.) The sun, namely, not only in the form of its body, but in the greatness of its light, because it lights up the lesser luminary and the stars. It is greater also in heat, because it warms the world, whereas in the days past before its creation it had had no heat. But that the moon is discerned to be of equal and of one greatness 76Cwith the sun, they say comes of this, that the sun goes far more distant from the lands and higher than the moon, and therefore the sun's greatness cannot be discerned by us. For all things set farther off appear smaller.

« And the lesser luminary, » etc. And although it sometimes happens that the moon and the greater stars are seen by day, yet it is agreed that they bring the solace of their light not to the day, but to the night.

(AUG., de Gen. ad litt. lib. II, c. 15, tom. III.) For then, at the beginning of the night, the moon is seen, when she is full, at other times not: and by day she begins to be seen before her fulness, and in the progress of the night.

VERS. 17, 18-- . . . . « That they might shine, » etc. These things can be understood of the great luminaries and of the stars; so that what is said, « that they might rule the day, » should belong specially to the sun, 76Dwhat is subjoined, « and the night, » to the moon and the stars; but what is added, « that they might divide the light and the darkness, » should suit all alike; which, wheresoever they go, carry light about them; whence, when they are absent, they leave all things dark. If it be asked what the daylight could have been like before the creation of the stars, we answer, such as we see when the rising of the sun is at hand, but it does not yet appear: when, namely, the day shines with the dulled rays of the stars, but the risen sun by no means blazes forth.

(ISID., in Gen., tom. V, col. 207.) On the fourth day the luminaries glittered in the firmament of heaven, etc., as far as because after good works there comes the illumination by which the species of the supernal truth is seen.

77A(AUG., de Gen. contra Manich., lib. I, c. 24, tom. III, col. 194.) On the fourth day, on which in that firmament of discipline the mind works and distinguishes spiritual understandings, it sees what is the unchangeable truth, which shines like the sun in the soul; and how the soul is made partaker of it, and bestows order and beauty upon the body, as the moon lighting the night. And how all the stars, that is, spiritual understandings, shine in the obscurity of this life as it were in the night.

VERS. 19.-- « And the evening and the morning were made, » etc. (BEDA, in Hexaem., tom. II, col. 13.) In this evening the people of God in Egypt offered the lamb, etc., as far as he might grant hope and faith.

« And the evening was made, » etc. (ISID., ubi supra.) 77BThe morning of the fourth day, the kingdom of David. This age is like to youth, which reigns among all the ages, and is the firm firmament of all the ages: whence it is compared to the fourth day, on which the stars were made in the firmament of heaven. The splendour of the sun indeed figures the excellence of the kingdom, the splendour of the moon shows the people obeying the king, as it were the Synagogue. But the stars are its princes, and all things founded on the stability of the kingdom, as it were in the firmament.

VERS. 20.-- « God also said, » etc. (BEDA, ubi supra.) After the face of heaven had been adorned on the fourth day with luminaries, the lower parts of the world, namely water and air, are adorned on the fifth day with their own living things.

« God also said: Let the waters bring forth the creeping thing, » etc. (AUG., lib. II de Gen. ad litt., ex c. 1, 2, 3, 4, 77C5 et 6, tom. III.) It behoved, in the creating of the inhabitants of the lower part, etc., as far as Which is not to be understood save of such as have been turned into the nature of the waters by the quality of a thicker air.

« Of a living soul. » (AUG., ibid., c. 8.) That is, the things which are in living souls, as it is said: "The ignoble things of men," that is, those who among men are ignoble. Which some think was said on account of their slowness of sense, or because fishes are of no memory; but it is most certain that they have memory.

(AUG., ubi supra, c. 3, 4.) Concerning the conversion of the elements there is a question. Some say that all things can be turned into all things, etc., as far as for that we feel the waters and the winds by touch; hence it is that the solid earth is mingled with all the elements, but in these thicker ones it is felt 77Dmore.

« Under the firmament » , etc. Even though there lies between an immense space, yet the birds which fly above the earth fly under the starry heaven: just as men who are on the earth are said to be under heaven. Another translation: « Beside the firmament of heaven, » because the spaces of this air which bears up the birds are neighbours to the ether.

(AUG., de Gen. ad litt., l, III, c. 9, 10, tom. III, col. 284.) It is to be noted that certain philosophers have so distributed the animals to each element, etc., as far as yet they have been permitted to hold only the misty places of the air, which is to them as it were a prison until the time of judgment.

VERS. 21.-- « And God created the great whales, » etc. 78A(THEOD. q. 18 in Gen.) Boys need now bugbears, now straps also and rods: by the former we frighten them, by the latter we instruct them: Since therefore God perceived that we too are prone to sloth, he founded the beasts, as it were certain straps and bugbears, that by frightening us with them he might draw us to himself, and that we might implore his help. But as those who are perfect despise bugbears of this sort, and straps; so those who have been brought up in virtue do not dread the onsets of beasts. Indeed the beasts stood by Adam before he had sinned, professing their obedience. And again the lion, the leopard, and all the fiercest of the beasts followed Noah himself as he entered the ark, after the manner of sheep. And the lions were present with Daniel, who, though otherwise famished, yet did not dare to approach him, because 78Bthey beheld in him the characters that bore before them the divine image. Likewise the viper, which had fastened its teeth in the hand of the Apostle, when it had found nothing in him tender or soft to sin, straightway leapt back and cast itself into the fire, exacting after a fashion punishment of itself, because it had attacked a body in no wise suited to it. But we for this cause dread the beasts, because we neglect virtue. Moreover the beasts are not useless to us for other things either: for out of them physicians compound not a few medicines for driving away diseases: let no one therefore, considering the beasts, complain of the Creator, but let him search out their usefulness.

« And every soul, » etc. In distinction from man, who was to be made to the image of God, and, if he kept the commandments, would remain in perpetual unchangeableness. 78CBut the other animals were so made that some should yield to others for nourishment, or should fail through age.

VERS. 22.-- « And he blessed them, » etc. (AUG., lib. de Gen. imp., c. 15, tom. III.) He willed the blessing to avail unto fruitfulness, which appears in the succession of offspring, so that by that blessing by which they were created weak and mortal, they might guard their own kind by being born.

(CHRYSOST., hom. 7 in Gen., tom. IV, col. 66.) The blessing itself, therefore, is to be increased and multiplied in a great number and multitude. For because living creatures were animate, and willed to be perpetually, therefore he subjoined: « Increase. » For that word preserves them even unto the present day. « Increase and multiply, » etc. (BEDA, in Hexaem.) It belongs to both 78Dkinds of living things made out of the waters, that is, to fishes and to birds. For as all fishes cannot live save in the waters, so very many birds; which, if they sometimes rest on the lands and make their young there, yet live not from the land but from the sea, and use the sea more willingly than the land.

Mystically. (ISID. et AUG., ubi supra.) On the fifth day there were made in the waters the creeping things of living souls, that is, men renewed unto life through the sacrament of baptism. There were made flying things, that is, souls flying to the things above. On the fifth day the mind that has advanced begins to work in the actions of a turbulent world, as it were in the waters of the sea, for the profit of brotherly fellowship, and out of the corporeal acknowledgments which belong to the sea, that is, 79Ato this life, to bring forth the creeping things of living souls, that is, works which profit living souls; and great whales, that is, the strongest acknowledgments, by which the waves of the world are broken and despised; and the flying things of heaven, that is, voices preaching heavenly things.

VERS. 23.-- « And the evening and the morning were made, etc. (AUG., de Gen. contra Manich., lib. II, c. 23, tom. III.) Mystically, the morning is the passing over into Babylon, when by that captivity the people was lightly settled in a foreign leisure, etc., as far as because they were so blinded that they could not even know Christ.

VERS. 24.-- « God also said: Let the « earth bring forth, » etc. (BEDA, in Hexaem., tom. III, col. 13.) After the heaven was filled with stars and the air with flying things, which on account of its nearness is called heaven, etc., 79Bas far as under the name of four-footed beasts all things are comprehended except the beasts and the creeping things, whether tamed, or wild and of the fields.

« Let the earth bring forth, » etc. (AUG., lib. III in Gen., c. 11, tom. III.) It followed that he should now adorn the earth with its own animals. By saying three times, according to its own kind, etc., as far as And under the name of cattle and of beasts every irrational animal is wont to be comprehended. « In its own kind. » (AUG., ibid. c. 12,) These things are perhaps said according to kind, because they so arose that others are born of them, and preserve by succession the form of their origin. But of man this was not said, because one was being made, of whom the woman also was made. For there were not made many kinds of men, as of herbs and of trees; so that it should be said, according to its own kind, as 79Cif it were said kind by kind, that among things alike, and belonging to one origin of seed, they might be distinguished from the rest.

(AUG., ibid. c. 14.) Concerning certain minute animals there is a question, etc., as far as except that they were fore-sown in them potentially and materially.

(AUG., ibid. c. 15, 16.) Concerning venomous and destructive creatures it is asked, etc., as far as all which, rightly considered, are praised.
