(AUG., ibid., c. 17.) This also moves us, etc., as far as but let them not doubt to prepare the numbers of a godly fortitude for all things.

(THEOD., quaest. 18 in Gen.) Let us not despise the beasts, as being altogether unharmed by them, as though they were of no strength. Sometimes God permits two or 79Dthree out of many thousands either to be stung by scorpions or bitten by serpents; that we, fearing lest we should suffer anything like, may call upon God the Creator for help.

(AUG. in Gen., l. III, c. 13.) It is asked why there was not added here what was said of the animals of the waters: « And God blessed them, saying: Increase and multiply, » etc. Perhaps because what had been said of the first creature of a living soul, he left to be understood of the second, especially because in the works of this day he was about to say many other things. But herbs and trees, because they have not the affection of generating, he perhaps judged unworthy of this blessing. Which was of necessity repeated in man, lest anyone should think 80Athat there is sin in the office of begetting sons, as in fornication without wedlock.

VERS. 25.-- « And God made the beasts of the earth, » etc. (BEDA in Hexaem.) It is a change of order. Nor need we care in our own speech which of the creatures we name first in order, since God created all things together.

(ISID., in Gen., tom. V, col. 207.) On the sixth day the earth brings forth the living soul, when our flesh, abstaining from dead works, brings forth living shoots of the virtues, according to its own kind, that is, by imitating the life of the saints. Whence I Cor. IV: « Be imitators of me, » etc. We live according to our own kind when we imitate the saints as our neighbours. The earth brings forth beasts, that is, men growing proud in ferocity. 80BAnd cattle, that is, those living simply. And serpents, namely the crafty, and those who discern good from evil, and who as it were by creeping search out earthly things, through which they may understand the heavenly.

VERS. 26.-- « Let us make man. » (AUG., ibid., c. 19.) There is intimated the plurality of the persons of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit, and straightway the unity of the deity, when it is said: « And God made man to the image of God, » not the Father to the image of the Son only. For it would not truly be said, to our image; but it was so said: « God made man to the image of God, » as if it were said « to his own image. » But when it is said « to the image of God, » whereas it was said above « to our image, » it is signified that the plurality of persons does not bring it about that 80Cwe should believe in many gods, but that we should receive the Father, and the Son, and the Holy Spirit as one God.

« Let us make man. » (BEDA, in Hexaem.) It appears why it was said that the rest should be made, etc., as far as but that he might show that he had received back true flesh after death.

« And let him rule, » etc. (AUG., lib. III de Gen. c. 20.) It is also to be noted that, when he had said « to our image, » he immediately subjoined: « And let him rule over the fishes of the sea and the fowls of the air. » That we may understand, namely, that man was made to the image of God in this, wherein he excels the irrational creatures. And that is reason, or mind, or understanding, or if it be more conveniently called by another name, that, namely, wherein he cleaves to the truth that is to be contemplated.

80DVERS. 27.-- « And God created man to the image, » etc. (BEDA, in Gen., c. 1.) He who, not by imitating any of the saints whatever, but by gazing upon truth itself, works justice, so that he may understand truth itself and follow it: he has received power over all things, because everyone made spiritual, and like to God, judges all things, and is himself judged by no man.

« And God created, » etc. (AUG., ubi supra, cap. 20, 21, 22.) It is to be noted that, as in the creating of light it is said: « Let there be light, » and straightway there follows, « and light was made; » so when it is said: « Let us make man, » there is brought in, « and God made man to the image of God: » because that nature is intellectual, as that light was: and this is for it to be made, namely 81Ato acknowledge the Word through which it is made. etc., as far as but fore-sown materially.

« Male and female he created them, » etc. (BEDA, in Hexaem. ) Afterwards it is set forth more fully both whence and in what manner men were made, etc., as far as although virginity be preferred.

(BEDA, ISID., ut supra. ) After these things « God made man to his own image and likeness, » a perfect man, namely, etc., as far as whence Paul: « We have sown unto you spiritual things, it is a great matter if we reap your carnal things (I Cor. IX). » [Vulg. 1 Cor. 9:11 Si nos vobis spiritualia seminavimus, a conditional, "If we have sown unto you spiritual things", closing as a question]

VERS. 28.-- . . . . . « And rule over the fishes, » etc. What he had said before of them as it were by counselling, he now says commanding, so that man may have, namely, dominion over all the creatures in the lower part of the world. Which he follows out 81Bone by one.

« And rule over the fishes of the sea. » (BEDA, in Hexaem. ) It is asked with what usefulness man received dominion among the other living things, etc., as far as and that the gaping of beasts and the venom of serpents gave way.

VERS. 29.-- « And God said: Behold, I have given, » etc. (BED., ibid.) It is plain that before the sin of man the earth brought forth nothing harmful, no poisonous herb, no barren tree. For all the herbs and trees were given to men, and to the birds, and to the living things of the earth for food. Whence it is plain that then animals did not live by the eating of animals, but fed harmoniously on herbs and fruits.

VERS. 30.-- . . . . . « And it was so done. » (AUG., de Gen. ad lit. lib. III, c. 13.) It is to be noted that where power is given to man of eating herbs and fruits, there is subjoined, 81C« and it was so done: » wherein it is signified that, when God said this, man knew these things to be granted to him for food, not that he ate them at once. For if we refer to all the aforesaid things what he says, « and it was so done, » it will follow that we say they were already multiplied on the sixth day, which was done after many years.

VERS. 31.-- « And God saw, » etc. (AUG., ibid.) It is asked: since the rest are severally called good, why is man, made to the image of God, said to be this along with the rest? But perhaps God foreknew that he would sin, and would not remain in the perfection of the image. For he who is good severally, is more so with all, but it is not convertible. It was therefore cautiously provided that there should be said what would be true in the present, and would signify the foreknowledge of the future. 81DBut God ordered all things so that, if any severally should by transgressing be deformed, yet always with them the whole should be beautiful.

« And the evening and the morning were made, » etc. (ISID., in Gen., tom. V, col. 207.) The morning of the sixth day is the preaching of the Gospel through Christ, etc., as far as which has no evening.

Morally. (AUG., de Gen. contra Manich., lib. I in fine.) On the sixth day the earth brings forth the living soul, that is, man, out of the stability of his mind, where he has spiritual fruits, that is, good thoughts; he governs all the motions of his soul, so that there may be in him a living soul, that is, one serving reason and justice, not earthliness and sin. So man is made to the image and likeness 82Aof God, male and female; that is, understanding and action: by whose coupling a spiritual offspring may fill the earth, that is, may subject the flesh, and the other things which have already been said in the perfection of man. For in these days the evening is the very perfecting of the several works, and the morning the beginning of those that follow.

CAPUT II.

VERS. 1.-- « Igitur perfecti sunt coeli et terra, » etc. (ALCUIN., in Gen. tom. I, col. 517.) Nullo modo dici potest quomodo fecit Deus coelum et terram. Sed haec expositio per ordinem dierum indicat tanquam historiam factarum rerum, sed maxime observat praedicationem futurorum, etc., usque ad quem Dominus utiliter latere monstravit.

82B« Igitur perfecti sunt coeli, et terra, et omnis ornatus eorum. » (AUG., de Gen. contra Manich., lib. I, c. ult.) Allegorice. Post illorum quasi sex dierum opera valde bona speret homo requiem perpetuam, et intelligat quid sit, » et requievit Deus die septimo ab operibus suis: » quia et ipse in nobis haec bona operatur, qui jubet ut operemur. Et recte quiescere dicitur: quia post haec omnia opera requiem nobis praestabit, quomodo paterfamilias domum aedificat, cum servientibus facere imperat; et post ab operibus requiescere, cum, perfecta fabrica, jubet quiescere.

(AUG., ibid.) Habet unusquisque nostrum in suis operibus et recta vita tanquam distinctos istos sex dies, post quos debeat quietem sperare: primo die 82Clucem fidei, quando primum visibilibus credit, propter quam fidem Dominus visibiliter apparere dignatus est. Secundo die factum est tanquam firmamentum disciplinae, quod discernit inter carnalia et spiritualia opera, sicut firmamentum inter inferiores aquas et superiores. Tertio die quo mentem suam ad proferendos bonorum operum fructus praeparat, et erigit, separata labe et fluctibus tentationum carnalium, tanquam aridam habet mentem a perturbationibus maris, ut jam possit dicere: « Mente servio legi Dei (Rom. VII), » etc.

VERS. 2, 3.-- « Complevitque Deus, » etc. (BEDA, in Hexaem. ) Alia translatio: « Consummavit Deus die sexto opera quae fecit, » quae nihil quaestionis affert, quia manifesta sunt quae in eo facta sunt, etc., 82Dusque ad aliquid enim operis fecit Salomon cum templum dedicavit.

(HIER., Quaest. Hebr. in Gen., tom. III, col. 935.) In Hebraeo habetur « die septima. » Arctabimus ergo Judaeos, qui de otio sabbati gloriantur, quia jam tunc in principio sabbatum solutum est, dum Deus operatur in sabbato, complens opera sua in eo.

« Complevitque Deus, » etc. (AUG., lib. IV de Gen. ad litt., c. 4, tom. III.) Quaeritur utrum, etc., usque ad secundum eum perfecta non fierent.

« Et requievit Deus, etc. (BEDA, in Hexaem. ) Non quasi operando lassus, sed ab universo opere quievit, quia novam creaturam facere cessavit. Requiescere enim cessare dicitur, ut in Apocalypsis IV: 83A« Requiem non habebant, dicentia: Sanctus, sanctus, sanctus, » id est, dicere non cessabant.

(BEDA, ibid.) Altius quoque intelligetur requievisse ab universo opere suo, etc., usque ad sed creaturis rationalibus in se requiem dedit.

« Et requievit Deus, » etc. (AUG., ubi supra, c. 8.) Non laboravit Deus in operando qui solo verbo fecit, dicendo: « Fiat. » Nec homo dicendo, « Fiat, » laborat. Sed forte dicitur laborasse cogitando quid fieret. A qua cura, perfectis rebus, quievisse dicitur. Sed hoc sapere desipere est. Deum ergo requievisse, est creaturae rationali in se requiem praestitisse; ut illuc scilicet desiderio feramur, quo requiescamus, id est nihil amplius requiramus. Sicut enim facere dicitur, quod ipso in nobis operante facimus, 83Bet cognoscere cum cognoscimus, sic requiescere, cum ejus munere requiescimus.

(Ibid., c. 11.) Non est mirum si diem quo Christus erat in sepulcro quieturus hoc modo praenuntians dixit: « Die septimo requievit, » deinceps operaturus ordines saeculorum.

(Ibid., c. 12.) Potest autem intelligi Deum requievisse a faciendis generibus creaturae, etc., usque ad et gubernare non cesset.

(Ibid., c. 14.) Deus autem nec creando defessus, nec cessando refectus est: sed per Scripturam suam ad quietis desiderium nos hortatur, dicendo se diem sanctificasse in quo requievit. Caeteros enim dies operis sui non legitur sanctificasse, tanquam apud ipsum plus quies quam operatio valeat.

83C(Ibid., c. 15.) Vitium quoque animae est ita suis operibus delectari, etc., usque ad ea requie a qua nunquam recessit.

(Ibid.) Si autem diceretur requievisse a faciendis, etc., usque ad sed ipso benedicti sumus.

(Ibid.) In se autem requievit Deus semper: in diebus vero quibus rerum consummatio narratur, in septimo tantum requievit, qui rerum sequitur perfectionem; a perfectis enim requiescit, qui perfectis non eget ut beatior sit.

(Ibid., c. 17.) Opera ejus videmus bona: quietem vero ejus post bona opera nostra videbimus. Ob quam significandam, unam diem sabbati praecepit observari: quod in tempore gratiae sublatum est, in 83Dqua perpetuum observat sabbatum, qui spe futuri omnia bona operatur, nec in bonis operibus suis, quasi non acceperit, gloriatur.

(Ibid., c. 18.) Apud illum quieti ejus nec mane nec vespera est, quia nec aperitur initio, nec clauditur fine. In perfectis autem operibus mane habet, et non vesperam; quia perfecta creatura incipit converti ad quietem Creatoris, sed illa non habet finem suae perfectionis, et sic requies Dei non ipsi Deo, sed rerum perfectioni inchoatur, et habet in eo mane quod ab ipso perficitur: sed in suo genere tanquam vespera terminatur, quod in Deo vesperam non habet, quia nihil erit perfectius ipsa perfectione.

84A(Ibid.) In illis autem diebus, etc., usque ad qui conditi ante luminaria memorantur.

(Ibid., c. 20.) Quaeritur quomodo dicatur in septimo, etc., usque ad nisi illa quae prius condita et dies vocata.

(Ibid., c. 21.) Sed quomodo circumire poterat lux, etc., usque ad et reliqua.

(Ibid.) Sed facilius est ut nos ignorare fateamur, etc., usque ad falsumque erit eum sexta die consummasse omnia opera sua.

(Ibid., c. 22.) Sed quoniam lux corporalis, etc., usque ad et illa qua creatura in seipsa noscitur recte vespera dicitur.

(Ibid., c. 25.) Quia vero angeli creaturam in seipsa sic sciunt, etc., usque ad Ideo septima die 84Bnulla vespera accessit.

(Ibid., c. 28.) Nec putet quis, etc., usque ad et verius mane?

(Ibid., c. 35.) Dies ergo ille primus, etc., usque ad et sanctificari ob hoc meruit.

(BEDA, in Hexaem., tom. II, col. 13.) Allegorice. Unde dicitur Exod. XX: « Memento ut diem sabbati sanctifices. » Haec autem benedictio et sanctificatio majorem benedictionem et sanctificationem significat, etc., usque ad Ideo haec dies vesperam habere non scribitur.

VERS. 4.-- « Istae sunt generationes. » (RAB.) Eos tangit qui mundum sine initio dicunt semper fuisse, vel qui a Deo factum putant, sed ex materia quam non fecerit, sed coaeterna ei.

84C« In die quo fecit, » etc. Diem ponit pro omni tempore, quo primordialis creatura formata est, etc., usque ad sine pluvia et opere humano.

« Istae sunt generationes coeli, » etc. (ISID., in Gen., t. V.) Numerati sunt sex dies quibus universitas saeculi a capite usque ad finem, etc., usque ad si non peccasset.

« Antequam oriretur in terra. » Id est, priusquam peccaret, id est terrenis cupiditatibus se subderet. Unde sequitur: « Non enim pluerat Deus super terram, » hoc est, nondum propheticis vel evangelicis nubibus, imbre verbi emisso, animam vivere fecerat, et « homo non erat qui operaretur terram, » quia post peccatum homo laborare coepit in terra, 84Dnecessarias habuit nubes illas, unde virgultum, id est anima virebat. Irrigabat eam fons vitae, id est, inundatio veritatis, loquens in intellectu ejus, ut pluvia de nubibus non egeret antequam peccaret. Hic erat status hominis ante peccatum.

(AUG., l. V, de Genes. ad lit., c. 1, tom. III.) Alia translatio habet: « Hic est liber creaturae coeli et terrae, » etc., usque ad magnos veritate, parvulos nutrit affabilitate.

(AUG., ibid., l. VI, c. 10.) In prima mundi conditione, etc., usque ad semen ex herba.

VERS. 5.-- « Et omne virgultum, » etc. (AUG., ibid., l. V, c. 4.) Intelligitur terra causaliter produxisse herbam et lignum, id est producendi virtutem accepisse. In ea enim tanquam in radicibus facta 85Aerant quae per tempora futura erant. Fecit ergo antequam essent super terram secundum formabilitatem materiae, quae formanda erant verbo ejus, praecedens formationem non tempore sed origine.

« Non enim pluerat Dominus, » etc. (AUG., ibid., c. 5.) Quasi non fecit Deus sic tunc sicut nunc, etc., usque ad nec creatura moveri posset.

VERS. 6.-- « Sed fons, » etc. (RAB.) Hujus fontis qualiscunque irrigatione jam terra herbis et lignis vestita supervenit. Fons unus ascendisse dicitur pro aliqua in terrae finibus unitate; vel singularis positus est pro plurali.

« Sed fons, » etc. (BEDA, in Gen., c. 2.) Hic intimare videtur quae fiant secundum temporum intervalla, etc., usque ad post per haec quae nota sunt 85Butcunque noscenda.

VERS. 7.-- « Formavit, » etc. (AUG.) Mystice. Latior de homine figurate explicatur narratio, etc., usque ad ut nec contemnat quod est, nec arroget quod non est.

« Formavit igitur Dominus Deus hominem, » etc. (AUG., de Gen. lib. III, cap. 20 et 25.) Prius de limo terrae formatum est corpus animale, etc., usque ad habet enim necessitatem moriendi.

(Ibid. c. 27.) Stola prima aut justitia est, de qua lapsus est: vel si corporalem immortalitatem signat, hanc amisit, cum peccando ad eam pervenire non potuit. Sic ergo Adam corpus animale habuit, non modo ante paradisum, sed et in paradiso (quamvis in interiori homine fuit spirituale) quod amisit peccando, 85Cet meruit corporis mortem, qui non peccando mereretur in corpus spirituale mutationem.

« Formavit, » etc. (AUG., l. VI de Gen. ad litt., c. I, tom. III.) Videndum est utrum recapitulatio sit, ut dicatur quomodo homo factus sit, quem sexta die factum legimus. An tunc cum fecit omnia simul, et hominem in his fecit, ut accessu temporis etiam hoc modo fieret, quo in hac perspicua forma vitam gerit? sicut fenum factum antequam exoriretur, accedente tempore, et fontis irrigatione, exortum est, ut super terram esset.

(AUG., ibid. c. 2, 3.) Secundum recapitulationem prius videamus, etc., usque ad tunc autem invisibiliter, potentialiter et causaliter quomodo fiunt futura, non facta.

85D« Formavit, » etc. (BEDA, in Hexaem. ) Factura hominis, etc., usque ad in qua viveret, creavit.

(AUG., de Gen. ad litt., l. VI, c. 13 et 17.) Quaeritur utrum Deus repente hominem in aetate virili fecerit, etc., usque ad et ideo necessario futurum erat.

Et inspiravit in faciem ejus spiraculum vitae, et factus est homo in animam viventem. » (AUG., ibid., l. VII, c. 3, 5, 7.) Sine dubitatione, « flavit, vel sufflavit, » dicendum est, etc., usque ad poenam si contemneret?

(Ibid., c. 26.) Dicendum est, etc., usque ad dubitare fas non est.

(Ibid., c. 27.) Quaeri solet utrum, si nolit incorporari, compellatur, etc., usque ad fecit eos.

86A(Ibid., l. VI, c. 7.) Sed forte animae sexta die factae, ubi imago Dei relucet, etc., usque ad quae praeponenda est in hujusmodi.

(IRENAEUS.) Aliud est habitus vitae qui animalem efficit hominem, aliud Spiritus vivificans, qui spiritualem hominem operatur. Unde Isaias: Qui dedit flatum populis super terram, et spiritum calcantibus eam; ut Deus flatum quidem omni populo qui est super terram dederit; spiritum vero his tantum qui terrena desideria calcant: ut flatus ad tempus sit, spiritus vero aeternus, ut pote qui proprie Dei donum sit.

VERS. 8.-- « Plantaverat autem, » etc. (BEDA, in Hexaem., tom. III.) Ab illo principio plantavit paradisum, etc., usque ad et quia de ligno vitae et scientiae 86Bboni et mali erat specialiter dicturus.

« Plantavit Dominus paradisum in Eden ad Orientem, » etc. (AUG., de Gen. ad litt., lib. VIII, c. 1.) Tres generales sententiae de paradiso sunt. Una eorum qui corporaliter tantum intelligi volunt, alia eorum qui spiritualiter tantum, tertia vero eorum qui utroque modo paradisum accipiunt. Tertiam mihi placere fateor: ut homo factus ex limo, quod corpus humanum est, in paradiso corporali locatus intelligatur. Adam, etsi aliud significat, quia est forma futuri, in natura propria homo accipitur.

(Ibid.) « Plantavit autem Dominus paradisum in Eden. » Id est in deliciis. Deinde hoc recapitulat ut ostendat quomodo paradisum Deus plantavit. « Et ejecit Deus de terra omne lignum, » etc. Jam enim 86Ctunc produxerat terra omne lignum, die scilicet tertio. Sed cum ex his generibus sint ligna instituta in paradiso, produxit in manifesto, et in tempore suo, quae jam causaliter tertio die terra produxerat.

(STRAB.) Quidam codices habent: « Eden ad Ortum. » Ex quo possumus conjicere paradisum in Oriente situm. Ubicunque autem sit, scimus eum terrenum esse, et interjecto Oceano, et montibus oppositis, remotissimum a nostro orbe, in alto situm, pertingentem usque ad lunarem circulum; unde aquae diluvii illuc minime pervenerunt.

« In quo posuit. » (AUG., ubi supra, cap. 5.) Si quis putat animas corpore exutas locis corporalibus contineri, etc., usque ad cujus una collyride Deus hominem a fame quadraginta diebus liberavit.

86DVERS. 9.-- « Produxitque Dominus lignum etiam vitae. » (AUG., Ibid., c. 4.) De sapientia dicitur: « Lignum vitae his est qui apprehenderint eam. » Sed est Hierusalem aeterna in coelis, etc., usque ad quae res suis temporibus gestas narravit.

« Lignum etiam vitae, » etc. (BEDA, in Hexaem., tom. III, col. 13.) In altero hominis signum obedientiae quam debebat: in altero sacramentum vitae aeternae, quam obediendo mereretur. Lignum vitae dictum, quia divinitus accepit, ut qui ex eo manducaret, corpus ejus stabili sanitate firmaretur, nec ulla infirmitate vel aetate in deterius vel in occasum laberetur.

(STRAB.) Historice. Lignum vitae hanc naturaliter 87Avirtutem habebat, ut qui ex ejus fructu comederet, perpetua soliditate vestiretur, nulla infirmitate, vel anxietate, vel senii lassitudine, vel imbecillitate fatigandus.

« Lignumque scientiae boni et mali. » (AUG. ubi supra, c. 6) Erat corporale sicut aliae arbores, nec cibo noxium, etc., usque ad quae transgressionem secuta est.

« Lignum scientiae boni et mali. » (AUG., lib. II de Gen. contra Manich., c. 10.) Animae medietas et ordinata integritas signatur, quod in medio paradisi plantatum, et scientiae boni et mali dictum: quia anima, quae debet in Deum extendi, si Deo deserto ad se conversa fuerit, et potentia sua sine Deo frui voluerit, poena sequente experiendo discit quid 87Bsit inter bonum quod deseruit, et malum quo cecidit: et hoc erit ei gustasse de ligno scientiae boni et mali.

VERS. 10, 11, 12, 13, 14.-- « Et fluvius egrediebatur. » (BED., ubi supra.) Ad irrigandum paradisum, etc., usque ad et paulo post emergentia solitum agere cursum.

(AUG., ubi supra, c. 7.) Alia translatio: « Fons exiit de Eden, qui irrigat paradisum, » etc. Haec flumina gentibus, etc., usque ad ligna scilicet pulchra et fructuosa.

(ISID., AUG.) Mystice. Fluvius de paradiso egrediens, affluentia est aeternae jucunditatis, etc., usque ad adversa tolerando.

(GREG.) Moraliter. Quatuor fluminibus de paradiso 87Cegredientibus terra irrigatur. Solidum rationis nostrae aedificium, prudentia, temperantia, fortitudo, justitia, continent: quia his quatuor virtutibus tota boni operis structura consurgit. Quatuor flumina paradisum irrigant, quia dum his quatuor virtutibus cor infunditur, ab omni desideriorum carnalium aestu temperatur.

« Evilath. » Regio Indiae quae post diluvium possessa est ab Evila filio Jectan, filii Heber, patriarchae Hebraeorum. Evilath interpretatur parturiens, quia aliquis loquens veritatem, magis parturit quam pariat. Plinius dicit regiones Indiae prae caeteris, venis aureis abundare.

« Bdellium. » Secundum Plinium arbor est aromatica magnitudine oleagina, cujus lacryma lucida, 87Dgustu amara, boni odoris, sed odoratior infusione vini.

« Onyx. » Est lapis pretiosus, sic dictus quia permistum habet candorem ad humani similitudinem unguis, quia unguis Graece dicitur ὄνυξ. Antiqua translatio habet « carbunculum et prasium. » Carbunculus est ignei coloris, et dicitur illustrare tenebras noctis. Prasius est viridis, unde Graece a porro, quod πράσον dicitur, nomen accepit.

VERS. 15.-- « Tulit ergo Dominus Deus, » etc. (AUG., ubi supra, c. 8.) Hominem in paradiso positum jam dixit. Nunc recapitulat ad quid positus est, « ut operaretur, » etc. Non est enim credibile quod Deus eum ante peccatum ad laborem damnaverit, 88Aetc., usque ad unde Eccli. X: « initium enim superbiae hominis est apostatare a Deo. »

« Tulit ergo Dominus Deus hominem, » etc. (GREG., lib. XIX Moral., cap. 17, tom. I.) Pensandum est quia bona prodesse nequeunt, si mala non caventur quae subrepunt, etc., usque ad sed sub ipsis superbiendo succumbunt.

« Et sumpsit Dominus Deus, » etc. (HIERON. Quaest. Hebr. in Gen., tom. III.) Pro « voluptate » in Hebraeo habetur « Eden. » Ipsi ergo LXX nunc « Eden » interpretati sunt, nunc « voluptatem. » Symmachus vero, qui « paradisum florentem ante transtulerat, hic « amoenitatem, » vel « delicias » posuit.

« Et posuit eum, » etc. (ISID.) Mystice. Ex eo quod additum est, « Et custodiret, innuitur qualis operatio 88Besset: quia in tranquillitate beatae vitae, ubi mors non est, omnis operatio est custodire quod tenes. Datum est enim praeceptum, ut de omni ligno paradisi sumerent, sed non de ligno scientiae boni et mali: id est non sic eo fruerentur, ut integritatem naturae suae usurpando vetitum violarent.

VERS. 16.-- « Praecipitque ei, » etc. (AUG., ubi supra, c. 13.) A ligno prohibitus est, etc., usque ad quid sit inter bonum obedientiae et malum inobedientiae.

(AUG., ubi supra, c. 17.) Quaeritur si viro soli, vel etiam feminae, etc., usque ad « domi viros suos interrogent. »

(PROCOP. in Gen. ) Dicunt aliqui: Si immortalitate nos amicire voluit, quid opus erat praecepto, propter 88Cquod peccaturi eramus et deinde judicii ejus sentire severos aculeos? Respondeo: Cum sit benignus et mansuetus, et vellet in incorruptibilem et immortalem conditionem asserere humanum genus: ne suae vel propriae virtuti beneficium id accepto ferrent, in hunc modum egit, voluit ut gratias ei haberent, his etiam destinavit praesentem vitam, ut palaestram et quasi doctrinam futurae vitae. Deinde inferens mortem ob peccatum, voluit ut agnosceremus nostram infirmitatem, et deinde nos ipsi magis appeteremus futurum donum.

« Praecepitque ei dicens: Ex omni, etc. (AUG. de Gen. ad litt., l. VIII, 28, tom. III.) Si quaeritur quomodo ista locutus sit, non proprie a nobis potest comprehendi. Certum est enim aut per suam substantiam 88Dloqui, etc., usque ad sed per subditam sibi creaturam.

Quaeritur quomodo loqui potuerunt, vel loquentem intelligere, qui non didicerant inter loquentes crescendo, vel magisterio? Sed eos Deus tales fecerat qui possent loqui, et discere ab aliis si essent.

(AUG., ubi supra, c. 14.) De Christo dicitur, Isa. VII: « Priusquam sciat puer bonum aut malum, contemnet malitiam, ut eligat bonum. » Quomodo quod nescit aut contemnit, aut eligit? etc., usque ad quod amittere non debebat.

VERS. 17.-- « De ligno autem, » etc. (AUG., ubi supra, c. 15.) Lignum ex eo, etc., usque ad id est, quod amabant non amissuri.

(SEVERIANUS.) Tres arborum differentiae erant in 89Aparadiso. Una quidem data illi erat ut viveret, altera ut bene viveret, tertia ut semper viveret.

« In quocunque enim die, » etc. Non ait, Si comederis, mortalis eris, sed « morte morieris. » Mortuus est enim homo in anima cum peccavit, quia recessit ab illo Deus, qui est vita animae: quam secuta est mors corporis, discedente ab illo anima, quae est vita corporis; quae Adae evenit, cum praesentem vitam finivit. Potest ita intelligi quod quando peccaverunt, statim morte illa puniti sunt, de qua dicitur Rom. septimo: « Infelix ego homo! quis me liberabit de corpore mortis hujus? » et ibidem octavo: « Corpus mortuum est propter peccatum; » non ait mortale, sed « mortuum, » quamvis mortale, quia moriturum. Non credendum est ante peccatum ita 89Bfuisse illa corpora, sed animalia nondum spiritualia, non tamen mortua, quae scilicet necesse esset mori, quod in die praevaricationis factum est.

« Morte morieris. » Duplex mors significatur: animae, Domino discedente, qui est ejus vita; vel corporis, quam in fine vitae accepit. Sed statim necessitatem moriendi incurrit.

VERS. 18.-- « Dixit quoque Dominus, » etc. (AUG., l. IX de Gen., c. 8.) Qui sentiunt ad gignendos filios in paradiso misceri non licere, etc., usque ad anima ex anima, sive alio modo fiat anima.

VERS. 19.-- « Adduxit ea, » etc. (AUG., l. IX de Gen. ad litt., c. 14, tom. III.) Non sicut venatores, vel aucupes; nec jussionis vox de nube facta est, quam rationales animae intelligere solent; hanc enim 89Cnaturaliter non intelligunt bestiae, vel aves, etc., usque ad terra esse creata intelliguntur.

(HIERON.) Videtur primam linguam humano generi fuisse Hebraeam, quia nomina quae usque ad divisionem linguarum in Genesi legimus, constat esse illius.

VERS. 20.-- « Appellavitque, » etc. (AUG.) Mystice. Significans gentes quae salvae fierent in Ecclesia per Christum, nomen Christi accepturas, quod prius non habebant; unde, Isa. LXV: « Vocabo servos meos nomine alio. »

« Adae vero non inveniebatur, » etc. (AUG., lib. IX de Gen. ad litt., cap. 15 et seq.) Videndum est quomodo facta sit mulieris formatio, quae mystice dicitur aedificatio, etc., usque ad ut intelligamus per illam extasim Adam divinitus haec dixisse.

89D« Adae vero non inveniebatur adjutor. » Nullus fidelis justus Christo aequari potest. Psalm. XLIV: « Speciosus enim forma prae filiis hominum. » Nemo enim poterat genus humanum liberare nisi ipse. Unde Apocalypsis V: « Nemo inventus est dignus aperire librum et solvere signacula ejus, » etc.

« Adae vero non inveniebatur, » etc. (AUG., de Gen. contra Manich., l. II, c. 13.) Facta est mulier in adjutorium viri, etc., usque ad et dominio rationis cooperante gratia subjugare.

VERS. 21.-- « Immisit ergo Dominus, » etc. (ISID. ex Aug., ut supra, c. 13, 14.) Mystice. Non possunt haec corporeis oculis videri. Sed quanto quis a visibilibus ad secreta intelligentiae quasi obdormiendo 90Asecesserit, melius et sincerius videt, etc., usque ad et Ecclesiae referenda.

(AUG., lib. X de Gen. ad litt., c. I.) Qui putant animam ex anima, etc., usque ad quam de viro fuerat dictum.

(AUG., ibid., c. 19, 20.) Si quaeratur unde accepit animam Christus, mallem audire meliores et doctiores; etc., usque ad quibus quid responderi possit, nondum mihi occurrit.

« Immisit ergo Dominus soporem, » etc. (GREG., Moral., l. XXX, tom. II.) Culmen contemplationis non attingimus, si non ab exterioris curae oppressione cessemus: nec nos ipsos intuemur, ut sciamus in nobis aliud esse rationale quod regit, aliud animale quod regitur, nisi ad secretum silentii recurrentes, 90Bab exteriori perturbatione sopiamur. Quod bene Adam dormiens figuravit, de cujus mox latere mulier processit. Qui enim ad interiora intelligenda rapitur, a rebus visibilibus oculos claudit, et tunc intelligit in seipso aliud esse quod regere debeat, tanquam vir; aliud quod regatur, tanquam femina.

VERS. 22, 24.-- « Et aedificavit. . . . Quamobrem relinquet, » etc. (ISID., in Gen., t. V.) Quod per historiam completum est in Adam, etc., usque ad Serpens enim haereticorum venena praesignat.

VERS. 25.-- « Erat autem uterque nudus. » (AUG.) Si Dei verbis vel cujuslibet prophetae aliquid dicitur, quod ad litteram absurdum videtur et ideo figurate dictum, ob significationem tamen dictum esse non est dubitandum. Corpora vero duorum hominum 90Cin paradiso nuda erant, nec erubescebant, quia nullam legem in membris senserant legi mentis repugnantem, quae inobedientiam secuta est. Nihil putabant velandum, quia nihil senserant refrenandum.

(AUG., de Civ. Dei, l. XIV, c. 17, tom. VII, col. 425.) Merito libidinis maxime pudet, etc., usque ad reddiditque confusos.

(RUPERT.) Non erat hoc ignominiae, sed honoris, quod nudi erant: non erat insipientiae, sed securitatis, quod nuditatem suam non erubescebant. Quid enim? nunquid hoc a factore suo accepit, quod confusibilis et verenda est factura Dei? Aut nunquid confusionem hujusmodi natura, et non potius conscientia facit? Est quidem quasi in naturam versum 90Dhoc tormentum confusionis: verumtamen non ex conditione primaeva, sed ex juniore culpa origo ejus pullulavit. Etenim idcirco nuditatem nostram erubescimus, quia conscii nobis sumus quantam infirmitatem, imo quantam adversum nos rebellionem carnis nostrae portamus. At vero tunc sana erat cordis conscientia, quippe quam nulla sollicitabat oculorum concupiscentia; siquidem concupiscentia carnis poena est peccati, qua praecedens punitur superbia mentis. Deus, inquit Scriptura, fecit hominem rectum. Rectitudo autem hominis in eo est ut carni spiritus, ut pote inferiori superior, praesit et imperet: medius namque inter Deum et carnem suam rationalis spiritus hominis positus est, ut Deo pareret 91Aet carni imperaret. Quem ordinem quia prior ille turbavit et rupit, abjiciendo praeceptum Dei: subditus est, qui erat superior, spiritus infimae carni, ut justa poena inferiori subjaceat, qui superiori recte subesse contempsit. Hinc illa confusio faciei de conscientiae secretis ad publicos oculos prorumpens, et sub misera velamentorum solatia poenalem ignominiam ire compellens. Haec, inquam, infirmitas vel rebellio carnis nondum erat: bene igitur nudus erat uterque et non erubescebant, quia quod erubescerent non habebant. Illa nuditatis securitate perdita, nos ingemiscimus, eo quod gloria et honore spoliati et panniculis obvoluti simus. Nam ita vestiti vere incedimus nudi, etiamsi, quod impossibile est, sic vestiamur sicut vestiuntur lilia 91Bagri. Etenim nec Salomon in omni gloria sua vestitus est sicut unum ex his. Si, inquam, vestiri possimus sicut haec vestiuntur, sic quoque spoliati et nudi sumus. Unde Salvatorem exspectamus Dominum nostrum Jesum Christum, qui reformabit corpus humilitatis nostrae, configuratum corpori claritatis suae. Nunquid enim quando fulgebunt justi sicut sol in regno Patris sui, tunicis operientur et palliis? Non utique, sed ad gloriam factoris sui, quaecunque illis fuerint vasa misericordiae, mutuis cum gloriae aspectibus patebunt, foris fulgidi, et intus jucundi; corpore integri, et animo laeti; corpore, inquam, simul et anima beati.

« Erat autem uterque nudus, » etc. (ISID., in Gen., ex Aug. de Gen. contra Manich., lib. II, c. 15.) Quod 91Cnudi erant et erubescebant, etc., usque ad quia in consensu rea tenetur conscientia.

CHAPTER II.

VERS. 1.-- « Therefore the heavens and the earth were finished, » etc. (ALCUIN., in Gen. tom. I, col. 517.) In no way can it be told how God made heaven and earth. But this exposition, by the order of the days, sets forth as it were a history of things done, yet above all it attends to the foretelling of things to come, etc., as far as which the Lord showed to lie hidden to good purpose.

82B« Therefore the heavens were finished, and the earth, and all the furniture of them. » (AUG., de Gen. contra Manich., lib. I, c. ult.) Allegorically. After those works of, as it were, six days, works very good, let man hope for perpetual rest, and let him understand what it is, » and God rested on the seventh day from his works: » because he himself also works these good things in us, who commands that we should work. And he is rightly said to rest: because after all these works he will furnish us with rest, in the way that the master of a household builds a house, when he commands his servants to do it; and afterwards to rest from his works, when, the building finished, he bids them rest.

(AUG., ibid.) Each one of us has in his works and in an upright life these six days, as it were, marked off, after which he ought to hope for quiet: on the first day 82Cthe light of faith, when first he believes things visible, for which faith the Lord vouchsafed to appear visibly. On the second day there was made as it were a firmament of discipline, which distinguishes between carnal and spiritual works, as the firmament between the lower waters and the higher. On the third day, on which he prepares and raises up his mind to bring forth the fruits of good works, the stain and the waves of carnal temptations being set apart, he has his mind dry, as it were, from the disturbances of the sea, so that he can now say: « With the mind I serve the law of God (Rom. VII), » etc.

VERS. 2, 3.-- « And God finished, » etc. (BEDA, in Hexaem. ) Another version: « God completed on the sixth day the works which he made, » which brings no question with it, because the things that were done in it are manifest, etc., 82Das far as for Solomon did some work when he dedicated the temple.

(HIER., Quaest. Hebr. in Gen., tom. III, col. 935.) In the Hebrew it stands « on the seventh day. » We shall therefore press the Jews hard, who glory in the leisure of the sabbath, because already then, in the beginning, the sabbath was loosed, while God works on the sabbath, completing his works on it.

« And God finished, » etc. (AUG., lib. IV de Gen. ad litt., c. 4, tom. III.) It is asked whether, etc., as far as according to him they would not be made perfect.

« And God rested, etc. (BEDA, in Hexaem. ) Not as though wearied by working, but he rested from the whole work, because he ceased to make a new creature. For to rest is said to be to cease, as in Apocalypse IV: 83A« They had no rest, saying: Holy, holy, holy, » that is, they did not cease to say it.

(BEDA, ibid.) More deeply too he will be understood to have rested from his whole work, etc., as far as but he gave rest in himself to rational creatures.

« And God rested, » etc. (AUG., ubi supra, c. 8.) God did not labour in working, who made by a word alone, saying: « Let it be made. » Nor does a man labour by saying, « Let it be made. » But perhaps he is said to have laboured in thinking what should be made. From which care, things being finished, he is said to have rested. But to be wise in this fashion is to be witless. That God rested, therefore, is that he furnished rest in himself to the rational creature; that is, that we may be borne by desire thither, where we may rest, that is, may seek nothing further. For as he is said to make what we make by his working in us, 83Band to know when we know, so to rest, when by his gift we rest.

(Ibid., c. 11.) It is no wonder if, foretelling in this manner the day on which Christ was to rest in the sepulchre, he said: « On the seventh day he rested, » being thereafter about to work the orders of the ages.

(Ibid., c. 12.) But it can be understood that God rested from making the kinds of creature, etc., as far as and does not cease to govern.

(Ibid., c. 14.) But God was neither wearied by creating nor refreshed by ceasing: rather through his Scripture he exhorts us to the desire of rest, by saying that he sanctified the day on which he rested. For he is not read to have sanctified the other days of his work, as though with him rest avails more than working.

83C(Ibid., c. 15.) It is also a vice of the soul so to delight in its own works, etc., as far as by that rest from which he never withdrew.

(Ibid.) But if he were said to have rested from things to be made, etc., as far as but by him we are blessed.

(Ibid.) But in himself God has always rested: yet in the days in which the completing of things is recounted, he rested on the seventh only, which follows the perfection of things; for he rests from things perfected, who has no need of things perfected that he may be more blessed.

(Ibid., c. 17.) His works we see to be good: but his rest we shall see after our good works. To signify which, he commanded one day of the sabbath to be observed: which in the time of grace has been taken away, in 83Dwhich he keeps a perpetual sabbath who works all good things in hope of what is to come, and does not glory in his good works as though he had not received them.

(Ibid., c. 18.) With him there is to his rest neither morning nor evening, because it is neither opened by a beginning nor closed by an end. But in works perfected it has a morning, and not an evening; because the perfected creature begins to be turned to the quiet of the Creator, but that quiet has no end of its perfection, and so the rest of God is begun not for God himself, but for the perfection of things, and it has a morning in that which is perfected by him: but in its own kind it is ended as by an evening, which in God has no evening, because nothing will be more perfect than perfection itself.

84A(Ibid.) But in those days, etc., as far as which are recorded as created before the luminaries.

(Ibid., c. 20.) It is asked how it is said on the seventh, etc., as far as except that which was created first and called day.

(Ibid., c. 21.) But how could the light go round, etc., as far as and the rest.

(Ibid.) But it is easier that we confess ourselves ignorant, etc., as far as and it will be false that he completed all his works on the sixth day.

(Ibid., c. 22.) But since bodily light, etc., as far as and that by which the creature is known in itself is rightly called evening.

(Ibid., c. 25.) But because the angels so know the creature in itself, etc., as far as Therefore on the seventh day 84Bno evening came on.

(Ibid., c. 28.) Nor let anyone think, etc., as far as and more truly morning?

(Ibid., c. 35.) That first day, therefore, etc., as far as and for this it deserved to be sanctified.

(BEDA, in Hexaem., tom. II, col. 13.) Allegorically. Whence it is said in Exod. XX: « Remember that thou keep holy the sabbath day. » But this blessing and sanctification signifies a greater blessing and sanctification, etc., as far as Therefore this day is not written to have an evening.

VERS. 4.-- « These are the generations. » (RAB.) He touches those who say that the world always was without a beginning, or who think that it was made by God, but out of matter which he did not make, but which is coeternal with him.

84C« In the day that he made, » etc. He puts the day for the whole time in which the primordial creature was formed, etc., as far as without rain and human labour.

« These are the generations of the heaven, » etc. (ISID., in Gen., t. V.) There are numbered six days in which the whole of the age from beginning to end, etc., as far as if he had not sinned.

« Before it should spring up in the earth. » That is, before he sinned, that is, before he made himself subject to earthly desires. Whence there follows: « For God had not rained upon the earth, » that is, he had not yet, by prophetic or evangelical clouds, the shower of the word being sent forth, made the soul to live, and « there was no man to till the earth, » because after sin man began to labour on the earth, 84Dhe had need of those clouds, whence the shrub, that is the soul, grew green. The fountain of life watered it, that is, the flood of truth, speaking in his understanding, so that it needed no rain from the clouds before he sinned. This was the state of man before sin.

(AUG., l. V, de Genes. ad lit., c. 1, tom. III.) Another version has: « This is the book of the creation of heaven and earth, » etc., as far as it nourishes the great with truth, the little ones with affability.

(AUG., ibid., l. VI, c. 10.) In the first founding of the world, etc., as far as seed from the herb.

VERS. 5.-- « And every shrub, » etc. (AUG., ibid., l. V, c. 4.) The earth is understood to have brought forth herb and tree causally, that is, to have received the power of bringing forth. For in it, as in roots, had been made 85Athe things that were to be through the times to come. He made them, therefore, before they were upon the earth, according to the formability of matter — the things that were to be formed by his word — preceding the forming not in time but in origin.

« For the Lord had not rained, » etc. (AUG., ibid., c. 5.) As though God did not then make in the way he now makes, etc., as far as nor could the creature be moved.

VERS. 6.-- « But a fountain, » etc. (RAB.) By the watering of this fountain, of whatever kind it was, the earth came on already clothed with herbs and trees. One fountain is said to have gone up, for some unity within the bounds of the earth; or the singular is put for the plural.

« But a fountain, » etc. (BEDA, in Gen., c. 2.) Here he seems to intimate what things come to be according to the intervals of times, etc., as far as afterwards, through these things that are known, 85Bto be known in whatever measure.

VERS. 7.-- « He formed, » etc. (AUG.) Mystically. A broader narrative concerning man is unfolded figuratively, etc., as far as that he neither despise what he is, nor claim what he is not.

« The Lord God therefore formed man, » etc. (AUG., de Gen. lib. III, cap. 20 et 25.) First out of the slime of the earth was formed an animal body, etc., as far as for it has the necessity of dying.

(Ibid. c. 27.) The first robe is either justice, from which he fell: or, if it signifies bodily immortality, this he lost, since by sinning he could not attain to it. So then Adam had an animal body, not only before paradise, but in paradise as well (although in the inner man he was spiritual), which he lost by sinning, 85Cand deserved the death of the body — he who by not sinning would have deserved the change into a spiritual body.

« He formed, » etc. (AUG., l. VI de Gen. ad litt., c. I, tom. III.) It must be seen whether it be a recapitulation, so that it is told how man was made, whom we read to have been made on the sixth day. Or whether then, when he made all things together, he made man also among them, so that with the accession of time he should be made in this manner too, in which he leads his life in this visible form? just as the hay, made before it sprang up, with time coming on and with the watering of the fountain, sprang up, so as to be upon the earth.

(AUG., ibid. c. 2, 3.) Let us look first according to recapitulation, etc., as far as but then invisibly, potentially and causally, in the manner in which things to come, not things done, come to be.

85D« He formed, » etc. (BEDA, in Hexaem. ) The making of man, etc., as far as he created that in which he should live.

(AUG., de Gen. ad litt., l. VI, c. 13 et 17.) It is asked whether God made man suddenly in the age of manhood, etc., as far as and therefore it was of necessity to come.

And he breathed into his face the breath of life, and man was made into a living soul. » (AUG., ibid., l. VII, c. 3, 5, 7.) Without doubt, « he blew, or he breathed upon, » is what must be said, etc., as far as a punishment if he should despise it?

(Ibid., c. 26.) It must be said, etc., as far as it is not lawful to doubt.

(Ibid., c. 27.) It is commonly asked whether, if it be unwilling to be embodied, it is compelled, etc., as far as he made them.

86A(Ibid., l. VI, c. 7.) But perhaps to the soul made on the sixth day, where the image of God shines back, etc., as far as which is to be preferred in matters of this kind.

(IRENAEUS.) The habit of life which makes a man animal is one thing; the life-giving Spirit, which works a spiritual man, is another. Whence Isaias: Who gave breath to the peoples upon the earth, and spirit to them that tread upon it; so that God gave breath indeed to every people that is upon the earth, but spirit only to those who tread down earthly desires: so that the breath is for a time, but the spirit eternal, inasmuch as it is properly the gift of God.

VERS. 8.-- « And he had planted, » etc. (BEDA, in Hexaem., tom. III.) From that beginning he planted paradise, etc., as far as and because he was about to speak specially of the tree of life and of the knowledge 86Bof good and evil.

« The Lord planted a paradise in Eden toward the East, » etc. (AUG., de Gen. ad litt., lib. VIII, c. 1.) There are three general opinions concerning paradise. One is of those who will have it understood only bodily, another of those who will have it only spiritually, but a third of those who take paradise in both ways. I confess that the third pleases me: so that the man made of slime, which is the human body, is understood to be placed in a bodily paradise. Adam, although he signifies something else, because he is the figure of him that was to come, is taken in his own nature as a man.

(Ibid.) « And the Lord planted a paradise in Eden. » That is, in delights. Then he recapitulates this, to show how God planted paradise. « And God sent forth out of the earth every tree, » etc. For already 86Cthen the earth had brought forth every tree, namely on the third day. But since out of these kinds the trees were established in paradise, he brought forth openly, and in its own time, what the earth had already brought forth causally on the third day.

(STRAB.) Certain codices have: « Eden toward the Rising. » From which we can conjecture that paradise is set in the East. But wherever it be, we know that it is earthly, and, with the Ocean set between and mountains standing over against it, most remote from our world, set on a height, reaching as far as the lunar circle; whence the waters of the flood by no means came thither.

« In which he placed him. » (AUG., ubi supra, cap. 5.) If anyone thinks that souls stripped of the body are contained in bodily places, etc., as far as by one cake of which God freed a man from hunger for forty days.

86DVERS. 9.-- « And the Lord brought forth the tree of life also. » (AUG., Ibid., c. 4.) Of wisdom it is said: « It is a tree of life to them that lay hold on her. » But there is an eternal Jerusalem in the heavens, etc., as far as which narrated things done in their own times.

« The tree of life also, » etc. (BEDA, in Hexaem., tom. III, col. 13.) In the one there was for man a sign of the obedience which he owed: in the other a sacrament of eternal life, which by obeying he would merit. It was called the tree of life, because it received from God that whoever ate of it, his body should be strengthened with a stable soundness, nor should it slip by any infirmity or age into the worse or into decline.

(STRAB.) Historically. The tree of life had naturally this 87Apower, that whoever ate of its fruit should be clothed with perpetual solidity, to be wearied by no infirmity, or anxiety, or weariness of old age, or feebleness.

« And the tree of the knowledge of good and evil. » (AUG. ubi supra, c. 6) It was bodily like the other trees, and not harmful as food, etc., as far as which followed the transgression.

« The tree of the knowledge of good and evil. » (AUG., lib. II de Gen. contra Manich., c. 10.) The mean of the soul and its ordered integrity is signified, in that it was planted in the midst of paradise, and called of the knowledge of good and evil: because the soul, which ought to be stretched out toward God, if, God being forsaken, it be turned to itself, and will enjoy its own power without God, then, punishment following, learns by experience what 87Blies between the good which it forsook and the evil into which it fell: and this will be for it to have tasted of the tree of the knowledge of good and evil.

VERS. 10, 11, 12, 13, 14.-- « And a river went out. » (BED., ubi supra.) To water paradise, etc., as far as and, emerging a little after, to hold their accustomed course.

(AUG., ubi supra, c. 7.) Another version: « A fountain went out of Eden, which watereth paradise, » etc. These rivers to the nations, etc., as far as namely trees fair and fruitful.

(ISID., AUG.) Mystically. The river going out of paradise is the affluence of eternal gladness, etc., as far as by bearing adversities.

(GREG.) Morally. By four rivers going out of paradise 87Cthe earth is watered. Prudence, temperance, fortitude, justice hold together the solid building of our reason: because by these four virtues the whole structure of good work rises up. Four rivers water paradise, because while the heart is flooded with these four virtues, it is tempered from every heat of carnal desires.

« Evilath. » A region of India which after the flood was possessed by Evila, the son of Jectan, the son of Heber, the patriarch of the Hebrews. Evilath is interpreted bringing to birth, because one who speaks the truth is more in travail than he brings forth. Pliny says that the regions of India abound in veins of gold beyond all others.

« Bdellium. » According to Pliny it is an aromatic tree of the size of an olive, whose gum is clear, 87Dbitter to the taste, of a good smell, but more fragrant with an infusion of wine.

« Onyx. » It is a precious stone, so called because it has a whiteness mixed in it after the likeness of a human nail, because a nail is called in Greek ὄνυξ. The ancient version has « carbuncle and prase. » The carbuncle is of a fiery colour, and is said to light up the darkness of night. The prase is green, whence it took its name in Greek from the leek, which is called πράσον.

VERS. 15.-- « The Lord God therefore took, » etc. (AUG., ubi supra, c. 8.) He has already said that man was placed in paradise. Now he recapitulates to what end he was placed there, « that he might dress it, » etc. For it is not credible that God condemned him to labour before sin, 88Aetc., as far as whence Eccli. X: « for the beginning of man's pride is to fall away from God. »

« The Lord God therefore took the man, » etc. (GREG., lib. XIX Moral., cap. 17, tom. I.) It must be weighed that good things cannot profit, if the evils that creep in are not guarded against, etc., as far as but by growing proud under them they sink beneath them.

« And the Lord God took, » etc. (HIERON. Quaest. Hebr. in Gen., tom. III.) For « pleasure » the Hebrew has « Eden. » The Seventy themselves, therefore, have rendered it now « Eden, » now « pleasure. » But Symmachus, who « had before translated it a flowering paradise, here put « pleasantness, » or « delights. »

« And he put him, » etc. (ISID.) Mystically. From that which is added, « And that he should keep it, it is hinted what kind of work 88Bit was: because in the tranquillity of the blessed life, where death is not, all work is to keep what thou holdest. For a precept was given, that they should take of every tree of paradise, but not of the tree of the knowledge of good and evil: that is, that they should not so enjoy it as to violate the integrity of their nature by usurping what was forbidden.

VERS. 16.-- « And he enjoineth him, » etc. (AUG., ubi supra, c. 13.) He was forbidden from the tree, etc., as far as what lies between the good of obedience and the evil of disobedience.

(AUG., ubi supra, c. 17.) It is asked whether to the man alone, or to the woman also, etc., as far as « let them ask their husbands at home. »

(PROCOP. in Gen. ) Some say: If he willed to clothe us with immortality, what need was there of a precept, on account of 88Cwhich we were to sin and then to feel the severe stings of his judgment? I answer: Since he is kind and gentle, and willed to lay claim to the human race for an incorruptible and immortal condition: lest they should set that benefit down to their own or proper power, he acted in this manner, he willed that they should render him thanks, and for them he also appointed the present life as a wrestling-school and, as it were, a schooling for the life to come. Then, bringing in death on account of sin, he willed that we should acknowledge our infirmity, and that thereafter we ourselves should the more desire the gift to come.

« And he commanded him, saying: Of every, etc. (AUG. de Gen. ad litt., l. VIII, 28, tom. III.) If it be asked how he spoke these things, it cannot properly be comprehended by us. For it is certain that he speaks either through his own substance, 88Detc., as far as but through a creature subject to himself.

It is asked: how could they speak, or understand one speaking, who had not learned by growing up among speakers, or by teaching? But God had made them such as could speak, and could learn from others if there were any.

(AUG., ubi supra, c. 14.) Of Christ it is said, Isa. VII: « Before the child knows good or evil, he shall despise wickedness, that he may choose the good. » How does he either despise or choose what he does not know? etc., as far as what he ought not to have lost.

VERS. 17.-- « But of the tree, » etc. (AUG., ubi supra, c. 15.) The tree from this, etc., as far as that is, that they were not about to lose what they loved.

(SEVERIANUS.) There were three differences of trees in 89Aparadise. One indeed had been given him that he might live, another that he might live well, the third that he might live always.

« For in whatsoever day, » etc. He does not say, If thou eat, thou shalt be mortal, but « thou shalt die the death. » For man died in his soul when he sinned, because God withdrew from him, who is the life of the soul: upon which followed the death of the body, the soul departing from him, which is the life of the body; and this befell Adam when he ended the present life. It can be understood thus, that when they sinned, they were at once punished with that death of which it is said in Rom. seven: « Unhappy man that I am! who shall deliver me from the body of this death? » and in the same, chapter eight: « The body is dead because of sin; » he does not say mortal, but « dead, » although it is mortal, because it is to die. It is not to be believed that before sin those bodies were so, 89Bbut animal, not yet spiritual, yet not dead — bodies, that is, which must needs die, which came about on the day of the transgression.

« Thou shalt die the death. » A twofold death is signified: that of the soul, the Lord departing, who is its life; or that of the body, which he received at the end of his life. But at once he incurred the necessity of dying.

VERS. 18.-- « The Lord also said, » etc. (AUG., l. IX de Gen., c. 8.) Those who hold that it was not lawful to have intercourse in paradise for the begetting of children, etc., as far as soul from soul, or in whatever other way the soul comes to be.

VERS. 19.-- « He brought them, » etc. (AUG., l. IX de Gen. ad litt., c. 14, tom. III.) Not as hunters do, or fowlers; nor was there a voice of command made out of a cloud, such as rational souls are wont to understand; for this 89Cbeasts or birds do not naturally understand, etc., as far as they are understood to have been created of earth.

(HIERON.) It seems that the first language of the human race was Hebrew, because the names which we read in Genesis until the division of tongues are agreed to be of that language.

VERS. 20.-- « And he called, » etc. (AUG.) Mystically. Signifying the Gentiles who should be saved in the Church through Christ, and should receive the name of Christ, which before they did not have; whence, Isa. LXV: « I will call my servants by another name. »

« But for Adam there was not found, » etc. (AUG., lib. IX de Gen. ad litt., cap. 15 et seq.) It must be seen how the forming of the woman was made, which is mystically called a building, etc., as far as that we may understand that through that ecstasy Adam said these things by God's gift.

89D« But for Adam there was not found a helper. » No faithful just man can be made equal to Christ. Psalm XLIV: « For he is beautiful in form above the sons of men. » For no one could deliver the human race but himself. Whence Apocalypse V: « No one was found worthy to open the book and to loose the seals thereof, » etc.

« But for Adam there was not found, » etc. (AUG., de Gen. contra Manich., l. II, c. 13.) The woman was made for the help of the man, etc., as far as and to subjugate to the dominion of reason, grace cooperating.

VERS. 21.-- « The Lord therefore cast, » etc. (ISID. ex Aug., ut supra, c. 13, 14.) Mystically. These things cannot be seen with bodily eyes. But the further a man withdraws from things visible to the secrets of understanding, as it were by falling asleep, 90Athe better and more sincerely he sees, etc., as far as and to be referred to the Church.

(AUG., lib. X de Gen. ad litt., c. I.) Those who think that the soul is from a soul, etc., as far as which had been said of the man.

(AUG., ibid., c. 19, 20.) If it be asked whence Christ received his soul, I would rather hear men better and more learned; etc., as far as to whom what answer might be made has not yet occurred to me.

« The Lord therefore cast a deep sleep, » etc. (GREG., Moral., l. XXX, tom. II.) We do not attain the summit of contemplation, if we do not cease from the oppression of outward care: nor do we look upon our own selves, so as to know that there is in us one thing rational which rules, another animal which is ruled, unless, betaking ourselves to the secret of silence, 90Bwe are lulled asleep from outward disturbance. Which Adam well figured in his sleeping, from whose side the woman presently came forth. For he who is caught up to the understanding of inward things closes his eyes to visible things, and then understands that there is in himself one thing which ought to rule, as a man; another which should be ruled, as a woman.

VERS. 22, 24.-- « And he built. . . . Wherefore a man shall leave, » etc. (ISID., in Gen., t. V.) What was fulfilled in Adam according to the history, etc., as far as For the serpent prefigures the poisons of heretics.

VERS. 25.-- « And they were both naked. » (AUG.) If anything is said in the words of God or of any prophet whatever which seems absurd to the letter and therefore to have been said figuratively, yet it is not to be doubted that it was said for the sake of its signification. But the bodies of the two human beings 90Cin paradise were naked, and they were not ashamed, because they had felt no law in their members warring against the law of the mind, which followed upon disobedience. They thought nothing was to be veiled, because they had felt nothing was to be restrained.

(AUG., de Civ. Dei, l. XIV, c. 17, tom. VII, col. 425.) Deservedly is there shame chiefly of lust, etc., as far as and it rendered them confounded.

(RUPERT.) That they were naked was not a matter of ignominy, but of honour: that they were not ashamed of their nakedness was not a matter of folly, but of security. For what? did it receive this from its maker, that the workmanship of God is shameful and to be blushed at? Or is it nature, and not rather conscience, that makes confusion of this kind? It is indeed as it were turned into nature, 90Dthis torment of confusion: nevertheless its origin sprouted not from the primeval condition, but from a later fault. For we blush at our nakedness for this reason, that we are conscious to ourselves how great an infirmity, nay how great a rebellion of our own flesh against us, we carry about. But then the conscience of the heart was sound, since no concupiscence of the eyes solicited it; seeing that the concupiscence of the flesh is the punishment of sin, by which the pride of the mind that went before is punished. God, says the Scripture, made man upright. But the uprightness of man lies in this, that the spirit should preside over and command the flesh, as the superior over the inferior: for the rational spirit of man was set midway between God and his own flesh, that it might obey God 91Aand command the flesh. And because that former man disturbed and broke this order, by casting away the precept of God: the spirit, which was superior, was made subject to the lowest flesh, so that by a just punishment he should lie beneath the inferior, who scorned rightly to be beneath the superior. Hence that confusion of the face, breaking out from the secrets of conscience to public eyes, and driving a penal ignominy to go beneath the wretched solaces of coverings. This infirmity, I say, or rebellion of the flesh, was not yet: rightly therefore was each of them naked and they were not ashamed, because they had nothing at which to blush. That security of nakedness being lost, we groan, in that we are despoiled of glory and honour and wrapped about with rags. For so clothed we truly walk naked, even if — which is impossible — we should be clothed as the lilies 91Bof the field are clothed. For not even Solomon in all his glory was clothed as one of these. If, I say, we could be clothed as these are clothed, even so are we despoiled and naked. Whence we look for the Saviour, our Lord Jesus Christ, who will reform the body of our lowliness, made like to the body of his brightness. For when the just shall shine as the sun in the kingdom of their Father, shall they be covered with tunics and cloaks? By no means, but to the glory of their maker, whatever vessels of mercy they shall have been, they will lie open to one another with mutual sights of glory, shining without, and joyful within; whole in body, and glad in mind; blessed, I say, in body and soul alike.

« And they were both naked, » etc. (ISID., in Gen., ex Aug. de Gen. contra Manich., lib. II, c. 15.) That 91Cthey were naked and were ashamed, etc., as far as because in consent the conscience is held guilty.

CAPUT III.

VERS. 1-4.-- « Sed et serpens erat callidior cunctis animantibus, » etc. (AUG., lib. II de Gen. ad litt. c. 2, 3, tom. III.) Alii, « prudentissimus, » translative dictum. Quidam codices habent « sapientissimus, » non proprie, sicut in bono solet accipi sapientia Dei, vel angelorum, vel animae rationalis, etc., usque ad sed potestas a Deo.

« Sed serpens, » etc. (STRAB.) Sic diabolus loquebatur per serpentem ignorantem, sicut per energumenos vel fanaticos: quemcunque enim arripit, intellectum et rationem tollit. Spiritus vero bonus quoscunque gratia repleverit, videntes et intelligentes 91Dfacit, unde spiritus prophetarum subjectus est prophetis.

« Qui dixit ad mulierem. » (AUG., lib. II de Gen. ad litt., c. 30, et ex capp. 7, 8, 6, 10, passim.) Inexcusabilis est praevaricatio mulieris, quia memor erat praecepti Dei. Oblivio tamen, etc., usque ad haec c. 10: Providet bonos et malos futuros, et creat: seipsum ad fruendum praestans bonis, multa etiam largiens malis.

« Cur praecepit, » etc. (STRAB.) Ideo callidus hostis interrogat hoc, et mandatum Dei memorat, ne cum peccaverit, excusationem habeat quasi oblita.

« Cur praecepit, » etc. (GREG. Moral., l. XXIII, tom. II.) Idem quotidie agit hostis humani generis: verba 92ADei de cordibus hominum molitur evellere, et minas leviare, et ad credendum quod falso promittit, invitat enim ad temporalia, ut levet quod Deus minetur aeterna supplicia. Spondens siquidem gloriam praesentis vitae, dicit: « Gustate, et eritis sicut dii, » et temporalem concupiscentiam tangite, et in hoc mundo sublimes apparete; et cum timorem divinae sententiae conatur amovere, quodammodo dicit: « Cur praecepit vobis Deus? »

(AUG., lib. II de Gen. ad litt., c. 4, tom. III.) Quaeritur cur Deus hominem tentari permiserit, etc., usque ad sive terrenum, ut hominis anima.

(Ibid., c. 5.) Nec putandum quod homo dejiceretur, nisi praecessisset in eo quaedam elatio comprimenda, ut per humilitatem peccati sciret quod falso 92Bde se praesumpserit, et quod non bene se habet facta natura, si a faciente recesserit. Commendatur enim quale bonum Deus sit, quando nulli bene est ab ipso recedere.

(Ibid., c. 18.) Haec quaestio de beata vita, etc., usque ad minus quam in vita sanctorum angelorum.

(Ibid., c. 16.) Non frustra putari potest ab initio temporis diabolum cecidisse, etc., usque ad quibus non dedit Deus praescientiam de seipsis?

(Ibid., c. 14.) Quomodo autem beati esse possint, quibus est incerta sua beatitudo? etc., usque ad mihi autem unde asserant, non occurrit.

(Ibid., c. 21.) Sed non frustra putandum est ab ipso creaturae exordio, etc., usque ad praevidens 92Cquanta de illo sua bonitate esset facturus.

(Ibid., c. 22.) « Initium autem figmenti » dicitur, etc., usque ad ut homines malos.

(Ibid., c. 23.) Quod ergo putatur diabolus nunquam in veritate stetisse, etc., usque ad plura in corpus ejus conveniunt.

(GREG., hom. 16, in Evang., tom. II.) Primum parentem diabolus tribus modis tentavit, etc., usque ad delectatione vincimur, consensu ligamur.

VERS. 5.--. . . . . « Eritis sicut dii, » etc. (STRAB.) Mystice. Artificiali dolo verba componit, ut si per inobedientiae contemptum subvertere nequiverit, saltem in hoc quod fidem corruperit, et idololatriam suaserit, victorem se glorietur: si autem per inobedientiae contemptum seduxerit, in 92Dutroque victor existat.

(AUG., de Gen. contra Manich., l. II, c. 15, tom. III.) Interrogata mulier respondit, etc., usque ad nullo enim regente per suam potestatem solus Deus beatus est.

VERS. 6, 7.-- « Vidit igitur, » etc. (ISID. ex Aug. ubi supra. ) Quomodo videbant si clausi erant oculi? Sed dictum est ut intelligamus eos oculos apertos fuisse, postquam de fructu comederunt, quibus se nudos videbant, et displicebant: id est, oculos astutiae, quibus simplicitas displicet. Cum quis enim ceciderit ab intima luce veritatis, nihil est unde superbia placere velit, nisi fraudulentis simulationibus. Hinc enim hypocricis nascitur, in qua multum 93Avidentur cordati, qui decipere potuerunt quem voluerunt.

« Vidit igitur, » etc. (GREG., lib. XXI Moral., cap. 2.) Ne in lubrica cogitatione versemur, non debemus intueri quod non licet concupisci, etc., usque ad exteriorum quoque sensuum disciplina servanda est.

« Deditque viro suo, qui comedit. Et aperti sunt oculi amborum, » etc. (ISID. ex Aug. ubi supra, c. 16, 17.) De quibus dictum est: « Tunc viderunt quod nudi essent, » oculis scilicet perversis, quibus nuditas, scilicet simplicitas, erubescenda videbatur. etc., usque ad « Ad meipsum turbata est anima mea. » (Psal. XLI.)

« Qui comedit, » etc. (AUG., de Gen., lib. XI, c. 3, tom. III, col. 429.) Ad concupiscendum scilicet 93Binvicem, etc., usque ad quae convinceret peccatorem facto, et doceret scripto lectorem.

« Consuerunt folia ficus. » (ALCUIN., in Gen. t. I.) Quia gloriam simplicis castitatis amiserunt, ad duplicem libidinis pruritum confugerunt; unde Joan. I: « Cum esses sub ficus vidi te, et descendi, » subaudi « liberare te. »

VERS. 8.-- « Et cum audissent, » etc. (AUG., l. II de Gen. ad litt., c. 33.) Ea enim hora tales conveniebat visitari, quia defecerant a luce veritatis, etc., usque ad corporalibus sensibus locali et temporali motu apparuisse credatur.

« Abscondit se, » etc. (AUG., ibid.) Cum Deus avertit intrinsecus faciem et homo turbatur, fiunt similia amentiae, nimio timore ac pudore, occulto quoque 93Cinstinctu non quiescente: ut nescientes facerent quae aliquid posteris significarent, propter quos haec scripta sunt.

« Ad auram post meridiem. » Unde hora nona Christus spiritum emisit (Matth. XXVII). Qui sexta hora fuit crucifixus, ut eadem hora restitueretur homo qua de paradiso est ejectus.

« In medio ligni paradisi. » Abscondit se, qui aversus a praecepto Creatoris, erroris sui et arbitrii voluntate vivit.

VERS. 9.-- « Vocavitque Dominus Deus Adam. » (AUG., ubi supra, c. 34.) Increpando, scilicet non ignorando, etc., usque ad nunc autem non significata referimus, sed gesta defendimus.

VERS. 10-11.-- « Qui ait: Vocem tuam audivi, » etc. 93D(AUG., ubi supra, c. 34.) Historice. Probabile est per congruam creaturam solere humanam formam hominibus primis apparere, etc., usque ad et secutum est quod puderet.

« Vocem tuam, » etc. (ISID., in Gen. tom. V.) Mystice. Respondet, voce ejus audita se occultasse, etc., usque ad et praecepto Dei non obedire.

« Vocavitque Deus Adam, » etc. (GREG.) Quia vidit in culpa lapsum jam sub peccato, velut a veritatis oculis absconditum, quia tenebras erroris ejus non approbat, quasi ubi sit peccator ignorat, eumque vocat ac requirit dicens: « Adam, ubi es? » Dum vocat, significat quia ad poenitentiam revocat; dum requirit, 94Ainsinuat quia peccatores jure damnatos ignorat.

VERS. 12.-- « Dixitque Adam: Mulier quam dedisti, » etc. (AUG.) Non dicit, Peccavi: superbia enim habet confusionis deformitatem, non confessionis humilitatem. Ad hoc autem scriptum est, et interrogationes ad hoc factae, ut veraciter et utiliter scriberentur, ut sciamus quanto morbo superbiae laborent hodie conantes malum in Creatorem referre, et si quid boni faciunt, sibi tribuere.

« Mulier quam dedisti, » etc. Quasi ad hoc data sit, ut non ipsa obediret viro, et ambo Deo.

« Mulier quam dedisti, » etc. (GREG., lib. IV Mor., c. 27, tom. I, col. 661.) Quatuor modis peccatum perpetratur in corde, etc., usque ad ad extremum falsae spei seductione, vel obstinatione miserae desperationis 94Bnutritur.

VERS. 13.-- « Et dixit Dominus Deus ad mulierem, » etc. (AUG.) Nec ista confitetur peccatum, sed refert in alterum, in impari sexu, pari fastu. Ex his tamen natus David dicit usque in finem saeculi: « Ego dixi: Domine, miserere mei, sana animam meam, quia peccavi tibi (Psal. XL). » Hoc ab istis dicendum fuit: sed nondum peccatorum cervices considerat Deus: restabant labores et dolores mortis, et contritio saeculi, et gratia Dei, quae in tempore subvenit, docens afflictos non de se praesumere.

« Serpens decepit, » etc. Quasi cujuslibet persuasio praevaleat Dei praecepto.

« Serpens decepit me, » etc. (AUG., ibid., lib. II, c. 30.) Quomodo his verbis, etc., usque ad cum eo 94Ccibo non videret eam mortuam esse.

(AUG., ibid., c. 28.) Notandum quod non diabolus est permissus feminam tentare, etc., usque ad sed ex hac homini invidisse ratio ostendit.

(ALC.) Peccatum angeli tacitum, hominis patefactum quia illud incurabile, hoc curatum.

« Vidit igitur, » etc. (STRAB.) Aperte inexcusabilis ostenditur, cui licuit sub tali deliberatione dolos inimici advertere.

VERS. 14.-- « Et ait Dominus Deus, » etc. (AUG., ubi supra, c. 36.) Tota haec sententia figurata est. Ubi credendum tamen omnia dicta esse. Serpens non interrogatur quare fecerit, quia non ipse propria voluntate et natura fecit, sed de illo diabolus, et per illum, et in illo: qui jam pro impietate et superbia 94Daeterno igni destinatus fuerat. Ad ipsum ergo refertur quod serpenti dicitur, et qualis humano generi futurus sit ostenditur.

Mystice. « Et ait Dominus Deus ad serpentem, » etc. (AUG., de Gen. cont. Manich., lib. II, c. 17.) Serpens non interrogatus prior excipit poenam, etc., usque ad ut ipsum initium suggestionis malae excludat.

« Super pectus tuum, » etc. (HIERON.) « Ventrem » LXX addiderunt, sed in Hebraeo habetur « pectus ª In Hebræo est gahon, quod ventrem et pectus significat. » tantum, ut calliditatem et versutiam cogitationum ejus aperiret, id est quod omnis gressus ejus nequitiae est et fraudis.

95A« Pectore et ventre repes. » (GREG., lib. XXI Mor., c. 2.) Malum luxuriae aut cogitatione perpetratur aut opere. Hostis enim cum ab effectu operis expellitur, secreta cogitationis polluere molitur. Ventre repit, dum lubricus per membra humana sibi subdita, luxuriam exercet in opere. Serpit pectore, dum polluit in cogitatione. Sed quia per cogitationem ad opera venitur, prius pectore et post ventre describitur repere.

VERS. 15.-- « Inimicitias ponam inter te et mulierem . . . Ipsa conteret caput tuum, » etc. (GREG. l. I Mor., c. 38.) Caput serpentis conterere, est initia suggestionis manu sollicitae considerationis a cordis aditu exstirpare; qui cum ab initio deprehenditur, percutere calcaneo molitur, quia quem prima tentatione 95Bnon percutit, decipere in fine tendit. Si autem cor in tentatione corrumpitur, sequentis actionis medietas et terminus ab hoste possidetur, quia totam sibi arborem fructus ferre conspicit, quam veneni dente in radice vitiavit.

(HIERON.) « Ipsa servabit calcaneum tuum, et tu conteres ejus calcaneum. » Melius in Hebraeo habetur: « Ipsa conteret caput tuum, et tu conteres calcaneum ejus, » quia et nostri gressus praepediuntur a colubro, et Dominus conteret Satan sub pedibus ejus.

VERS. 16.-- « Mulieri quoque, » etc. (AUG., ubi supra, c. 37.) Haec figurate et prophetice melius intelliguntur, etc., usque ad depravabitur natura, et 95Caugebitur culpa.

« Multiplicabo aerumnas, » etc. (AUG., de Gen. contra Manich., l. II, c. 19.) Manifestum est multiplicatos esse dolores mulieris, etc., usque ad sed prius reluctatum est cum dolore consuetudini malae.

VERS. 17-18.-- . . . « Maledicta terra, » etc. (AUG. ibid., c. 38.) Hos esse labores humani generis in terra nemo ignorat, et quod non esset, si felicitas quae erat in paradiso teneretur indubitanter constat.

(STRAB.) Terra maledicitur, non Adam, ut cuncti propter quos haec scripta sunt terrerentur, ne similia facientes, simili poena plecterentur. Ipse vero Cain, quia primae praevaricationi fratricidium addidit, maledicitur cum dicitur: « Maledictus eris super terram, » 95Detc.

(AUG.) Terrae maledixit, non aquis, quia homo de fructu terrae contra vetitum manducavit, non de aquis bibit, et in aquis erat peccatum abluendum de fructu terrae contractum. Animalia quoque terrestria plus maledictionis habent quam aquatilia, quia plus vivunt de maledicta terra. Inde Christus post resurrectionem de pisce manducavit, non de terrestri animali.

« Maledicta terra in opere tuo. » (GREG., Moral. lib. IV, c. 5, 6.) Deus maledicit homini peccanti, etc., usque ad et duos quinquagenarios flamma combussit.

« In laboribus, » etc. (RAB.) Spinae antea erant, sed non ut laborem homini inferrent: cui post peccatum 96Afuerunt ad laborem et afflictionem, sicut serpentes et quaecunque noxia, ante innoxia. Et notandum quod Adae extrinseca poena imponitur: « Maledicta terra in opere tuo; » mulieri vero intrinseca, cum dicitur: « Multiplicabo aerumnas tuas, » etc., quia seducta seduxit virum.

VERS. 19.-- « Pulvis es, » etc. (GREG., ubi supra.) Homo ita conditus fuit, etc., usque ad senectus transit ad mortem.

VERS. 20.-- « Et vocavit Adam nomen uxoris suae Eva, » etc. (AUG., ubi supra. ) Verba primi hominis haec fuerunt, quoniam haec est mater omnium viventium, tanquam reddat causam nominis a se impositi, cur scilicet Evam vocaverit.

Mystice. (AUG., de Gen. contra Manich., l. II, c. 21.) 96BPost peccatum et judicis sententiam vocavit Adam uxorem suam Evam, etc., usque ad in pellibus exprimitur quae mortuis corporibus detrahuntur.

VERS. 21.-- « Fecit quoque, » etc. (AUG., de Gen. lib. XI, c. 39.) Hoc quoque significationis causa factum est, et in factis quaeritur quid factum sit, et quid significet, et in dictis quid dictum sit, quidve significet.

(STRAB.) Ipsi fecerunt sibi perizomata, ut peccatum suum absconderent: Deus vero tunicas pelliceas, quibus totum eorum corpus induit, quia et in corpore et in anima eos juste damnavit. Stulta vero questio est qualiter vel quo operante pelles ab animalibus deductae sint, quaerere: qui enim de nihilo omnia condidit, quomodo et qualiter voluit, haec 96Cfecit.

VERS. 22.-- « Et ait: Ecce Adam quasi unus ex nobis, » etc. (AUG., ibid., c. 41.) Propter trinitatem pluralis numerus ponitur, etc., usque ad sicut in Ecclesia solent homines a sacramentis visibilibus Ecclesiae disciplina removeri.

(AUG., ubi supra, c. 42.) Mirum est si Adam spiritualis erat mente, etc., usque ad quae falsum verum esse putavit.

« Quasi unus ex nobis. » (AUG., de Gen. contra Manich., lib. II, c. 21.) Dupliciter intelligi potest, « quasi unus ex nobis, » id est Deus, etc., usque ad donec Dei misericordia reviviscat qui mortuus fuerat.

(RUPERT.) Ne comedat de ligno vitae, et hoc facto talis, tam miser factus Adam, vivat in aeternum. Quid 96Denim si cum talis factus sit vivat in aeternum? utique nil nisi malum aeternum. Jam enim miser factus, si aeternus quoque sit, quid nisi miseriam aeternam habebit? Parcamus illi, ne hoc modo male sit « quasi unus ex nobis; » ne sicut quisque nostrum aeternus est (aeternus enim Pater, aeternus Filius, aeternus Spiritus sanctus), sic et ille aeternus sit, et ob hoc veracem se esse rideat oculus nequam diaboli, qui Dei similitudinem ei repromisit. Etenim malum quidem illi est temporalem esse, sed pessimum esset aeternum esse. Mala nimis et falsa est haec similitudo Dei. Multo melius illi est esse omnino dissimilem Dei. Quomodo? Videlicet, ut, quia miser est, sit etiam temporalis, hoc est esse omnino dissimilem Dei. 97ADeus enim et aeternus est, et felix, et est ejus aeterna felicitas, felix aeternitas. Horum alterum, id est felicitatem, perdidit diabolus, aeternitatem vero non amisit; et est ejus aeterna infelicitas, infelix aeternitas.
